Andrew Feldman on Building Cerebras and the Future of Chips | Ep. 57

Uncapped with Jack Altman(28.2K)·62:45·2026-09-15
Andrew FeldmanCEO, Cerebras Systems Jack AltmanHost, Uncapped Eric VishriaGeneral Partner, Benchmark
TL;DRCerebras Co-Founder and CEO Andrew Feldman and Benchmark Partner Eric Vishria discuss the grueling 10-year journey of building the world's largest AI chip company, the Wafer Scale Engine, overcoming nearly fatal technical hurdles, and rethinking the ...
semiconductor supply chain to...

TL;DR

Cerebras Co-Founder and CEO Andrew Feldman and Benchmark Partner Eric Vishria discuss the grueling 10-year journey of building the world's largest AI chip company, the Wafer Scale Engine, overcoming nearly fatal technical hurdles, and rethinking the semiconductor supply chain to tackle incumbent giants like Nvidia.

The Genesis of Cerebras & The 100x Strategy

  • The 2016 Insight: As a computer architect, Feldman recognized that AI workloads would be exceptionally compute-intensive, requiring a radically different architecture rather than remodeled existing chips.
  • The Goliath Strategy: Feldman and Vishria argue that attacking an incumbent like Nvidia with a 10% or 2x better product is a losing game because incumbents possess massive margin flexibility to cut prices and bundle products.
  • The 100x Rule:
    • Scale timeline: A new compute company takes at least 5 years to reach scale.
    • Incumbent compounding: If an incumbent doubles every year ($2^5 = 32\text{x}$ over 5 years), a new entrant must target a 100x to 500x performance multiple to successfully intersect the market ahead of them.
  • Control Your Own Stack: To achieve radical innovation, Cerebras brought chip, board, system, software, and API development entirely in-house.
    • No Off-the-Shelf Parts: Building a chip the size of a dinner plate meant no commercial catalog items (like heat sinks or packaging tools) existed; they had to pioneer world-class packaging through years of failure.

The Valley of Death: Surviving the 18-Month Nightmare

  • The Boardroom Reality: For 18 months, Cerebras faced a continuous existential hurdle: burning $8 million a month while walking into board meetings every six weeks with the exact same update: *"Still can't make it."*
  • The Mantra: The team operated on a strict engineering discipline: "Only new mistakes." Every failure underwent rigorous analysis so they never repeated the same error twice.
  • The Breakthrough: In July 2019, after months of shattering wafers in seconds, then minutes, a test run maintained a flat temperature in a makeshift lab with a hole drilled in the wall for ventilation—solving a problem that had baffled computer architecture for 75 years.

Demystifying the Semiconductor Supply Chain

  • The Modern Pyramid (TSMC & ASML): A cutting-edge fab costs $40 to $50 billion, takes 4 to 5 years to build, and utilizes football-field-sized facilities powered by continuous concrete pouring.
  • The Lithography Monopoly: ASML builds the $500M+ photolithography machines (50–60 feet long, 20 feet high) that etch transistors into silicon wafers—representing a true technological monopoly.
  • The Geopolitical Bottleneck: Three decades of domestic policy pushed fabs and packaging vendors (like Amcor and ASE) overseas. Feldman advocates for waiving local ordinances to build robust US domestic fab capacity with partners like TSMC, Samsung, and GlobalFoundries.

Data Center Constraints & Energy Realities

  • The Real Estate Mismatch: AI moves at the speed of software while data centers move at the speed of real estate, creating a permanent lag.
  • Power and Grid Hurdles:
    • The US grid relies on 1940s-1950s technology.
    • Data center expansion is driving intense innovation in backup power, including jet-engine power adaptations (from Boom), fuel cells (Bloom Energy), and renewed interest in nuclear power.
  • The Water Fallacy: Data centers utilizing closed-loop water systems consume between 4x and 7x less water than California almond growers.

Scaling Throughput & Disaggregation

  • Speed Creates Markets: Just as fast internet transformed Netflix from mailing DVDs into a movie studio, instant frontier intelligence unlocks entirely new applications.
  • Disaggregation Partnerships: Cerebras leverages its architecture to partner with other chipmakers (such as AMD and AWS), achieving up to 5x additional throughput while maintaining constant speeds by splitting inference workflows.

Notable Quotes

*"The more you work on companies and you see the challenges and you see how hard it is you build up scar tissue... you actually need some naivety... like you need to have this like 'can do' attitude and this naive bet around it to attempt it."* — Eric Vishria
*"We're a professional David in the battle with Goliath and I I wake up every day with that mentality... We're only interested in solving problems that other people can't solve."* — Andrew Feldman
*"A fab that builds at cutting edge geometries is a modern pyramid. It's one of the greatest things humans make... out the other end comes a $22 chip. It blows your mind when you think about it."* — Andrew Feldman

Baseten CEO on why the AI boom is just getting started

Sources Podcast(18.4K)·48:17·32.3K views·2026-09-15
Tuhin SrivastavaCEO and Co-founder, Baseten Alex HeathHost, Sources Podcast
TL;DRTuhin Srivastava, CEO and co-founder of AI cloud startup Baseten, discusses the explosive demand for AI inference, the shift toward enterprise-owned intelligence, and how autonomous agents running in virtual machines are reshaping the software stack and infrastructure markets.

TL;DR

Tuhin Srivastava, CEO and co-founder of AI cloud startup Baseten, discusses the explosive demand for AI inference, the shift toward enterprise-owned intelligence, and how autonomous agents running in virtual machines are reshaping the software stack and infrastructure markets.

AI Inference and the Baseten Architecture

  • Definition of AI Inference: Serving post-trained, open-weight, or custom models in production, requiring complex underlying infrastructure, orchestration, and chip-level optimization.
  • The Three-Layer Problem:
    • Infrastructure/Compute: Procuring chips (like B200s and G300s) across a heterogeneous network of 20 different clouds and 90 regions.
    • Core Inference Layer: Providing performance, reliability, numerics, and software tooling behind an API.
    • Adjacent Primitives: Incorporating post-training (RL), sandboxes for code execution, evals, and routing.
  • Differentiation from Hyperscalers: Baseten focuses specifically on inference and production reliability, moving faster and offering more customer alignment than traditional cloud providers (AWS, Azure, Google Cloud).
  • Current Scale:
    • Processing 40 to 50 trillion tokens a day.
    • Token volume has grown 40x year-on-year.
    • Revenue has 10xed in the last 12 months with thousands of customers.
    • Valued at $5 billion in January 2026 and $13 billion in June.

The Rise of "Owned Intelligence" and Open Weight Models

  • Concept of Owned Intelligence: Enterprises want to control their own models, data sovereignty, and user feedback loops to protect margins and avoid reliance on closed frontier providers.
  • Market Shift in Open Source:
    • 18 months ago (around Deepseek V3's release in December 2024), enterprises were skeptical and fearful of open weights.
    • Today, capability gains have driven widespread enterprise adoption, and the origin debate has largely evaporated as open weight usage surges.
  • Base Labs: Baseten's internal research lab, established via a team acquisition, dedicated to pushing the open weight ecosystem and continual learning loops.

Vertical Integration and Market Growth

  • Driving Factors for Going Vertical: Moving up and down the stack is driven by the need to deepen customer value (learning loops) and secure raw compute capacity for future scale.
  • Future Compute Demand: Srivastava projects Baseten will be one of the largest individual compute consumers on the planet within 12 to 24 months.
  • The Agentic Shift & Blacksel Acquisition:
    • Baseten acquired Blacksel to integrate agent runtimes, virtual machine sandboxes, and browser-use environments directly with inference layers.
    • Agents (such as coding agents utilizing Baseten's MCP server and skills) are increasingly deploying and debugging models autonomously on the platform.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global AI Spend: Projected by industry figures to reach $5 trillion in 5 years, generating roughly $1 to $5 trillion in inference spend (Tuhin Srivastava).
  • American Open Weight Models: Truly competitive American open weight models will emerge in weeks, not months or quarters (Tuhin Srivastava).
  • Compute Volume Growth: Inference demand will scale 100x or 1,000x as consumer adoption of agents catches up to enterprise usage (Tuhin Srivastava).
  • Future of AI Markets: Post-AGI (or in a high-AI world), inference will become the primary or only market left because all models will run continuously in execution loops (Tuhin Srivastava).

Notable Quotes

"Until you have that thing, until you have that loop working, the compute's kind of useless. Or it is a... reselling of taking access to it and putting it somewhere else." — Tuhin Srivastava

Box's Aaron Levie: On Reinventing Yourself in the AI Age and Enterprise Diffusion

Sequoia Capital(252.0K)·65:22·11.0K views·2026-09-15
Aaron LevieCEO and Co-founder, Box
TL;DRAaron Levie, co-founder and CEO of Box, explains why the application layer is capturing massive enterprise value by bridging raw foundational AI models to bespoke workflows, and predicts that within five years, 90% of enterprise tokens will be executed autonomously for knowledge workers.

TL;DR

Aaron Levie, co-founder and CEO of Box, explains why the application layer is capturing massive enterprise value by bridging raw foundational AI models to bespoke workflows, and predicts that within five years, 90% of enterprise tokens will be executed autonomously for knowledge workers.

The Application Layer and the Model-Workflow Bridge

  • The Neo Labs Bet: Application companies are acting as the hottest neo labs, proving that a massive gap exists between raw LLM intelligence and real-world enterprise workflows.
  • Operational Reality Check: Research-pilled organizations often underestimate how intelligence is actually rolled out, which requires bridging connections to legacy data systems, human-in-the-loop interactions, workflow delays, and business change management.
  • The Infrastructure-Application Analogy: Similar to how AWS/GCP created infrastructure that enabled massive independent software value (e.g., Snowflake, Databricks), foundational models will spawn trillions in application value within banking, life sciences, healthcare, and government.

Token Economics and the "Fox Guarding the Henhouse"

  • Model-Agnostic Routing: Enterprises do not want token sellers (the labs) acting as the tollbooth metering and gating what is best for every use case; they prefer a model-agnostic layer that cost-optimizes across multiple models with accuracy held constant.
  • Temporary Subsidies: Token subsidization by labs is a temporary phenomenon that will hit gross margin and capitalist constraints as companies mature and face the true costs of training runs.
  • Divergent Ecosystem Players: Meta, SpaceX, China, and NVIDIA do not need the same inference margin structures as Anthropic or OpenAI, ensuring that per-token costs will continue to drive down toward the application layer.

Box's AI Reinvention and Agent Harnesses

  • The Box Agent: Box sits on hundreds of billions of files (contracts, research, marketing assets, loan documents) that enterprises historically could not search at scale; Box built an agentic platform to automate data extraction, metadata generation, and workflow steps.
  • Custom Agentic Harness: Box built an agent harness tuned directly to its file systems, permission structures, and search engine, which beats handing raw APIs to Claude or ChatGPT on both accuracy and latency.
  • Evals and Model Gardens: Box runs hundreds of tests on every model using a "complex work eval" (domain-specific tasks in life sciences, finance, public sector) and a private Box employee holdback eval, offering customers default models alongside a model garden.

Diffusion of AI in Knowledge Work vs. Coding

  • Why Coding Diffused Fast: Coding acts as a pure utility where 100% of value is correlated to generated text lines, engineers are technically savvy enough to debug failing MCP servers without calling IT, and code repos live in centralized places like GitHub.
  • The Knowledge Work Friction: Knowledge work is rate-limited by external factors (e.g., customer budgets, sales meeting schedules), lacks centralized data repositories equivalent to a GitHub repo, and enforces strict access controls (such as legal walls between cases).
  • The "Work Slop" Dilemma: Society is experiencing a messy transitional phase regarding AI-generated content in strategy decks and writing, where individuals use AI for brainstorming yet harbor trust issues and allergic reactions when receiving "work slop" from peers.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Token Automation Timeline: Aaron Levie predicts that within five years, 90% of enterprise tokens will go to work via automated background agents where no human kicks them off, and workers simply review the resulting task queue.

Notable Quotes

"The value isn't only in the model; it's in the bridge from a model's raw capability to the actual workflow inside a bank, a law firm, or a pharma company." — Aaron Levie
"The subsidization of tokens is a I think it just has to be a temporary phenomenon. When these companies become public, I think they will be held to basically the same laws of capitalism that everybody else is." — Aaron Levie

And this is not a one-off in history. We've always had this relationship between infrastructure and application. Obviously, AWS or GCP or Azure have created trillions of dollars in value of market cap of infrastructure, but guess what? There's also trillions of dollars of value in software that only exists because of that infrastructure. And if you were to go back 10 years ago and look at what was happening in the data space, and you looked at what early versions of GCP or AWS were building, I guarantee you would not have predicted Snowflake or Databricks existing. You would have been like, "The infrastructure just already does that. Why would you pay another $10 billion of revenue to all these other products that are just making it so you can work with your data?" The same thing is going to be true for intelligence, which is the models will be insanely valuable, but the application of bringing those models into real workflows in banking and life sciences and healthcare and government, that's just going to be a lot of software. Now, the challenge for the next, let's say, two to five years is how much do the model providers need to move up that stack and also try and compete at that layer, or do they either leave open, intentionally or accidentally, that entire space for the application ecosystem? And actually, to some extent, this is a big question strategically for them because, on one hand, you want to be closer to the customer; on the other hand, you also want to be able to have an ecosystem so people trust you. So, that's going to be a really interesting tension over the coming years. How do you think they'll play out? "I feel like that's the question we spend every day wrestling with." Yes. You can sense some of the existential dread of a VC right now. It's like, "Should I just put another billion dollars into Anthropic? Or should I attempt to see what the applied layer is going to play out?" So, nobody is that envious of your guys' position, having to figure that out. But obviously, it's even harder for the entrepreneur. But I am pretty long, obviously, on the application layer. I have a very concentrated bet, with very limited diversification, on it working out that you still want to buy technology that understands your workflow and can get to the core enterprise data. I just don't see a different event happening than all of history. I mean, we only have like 50 or 60 years of computer software history, but basically, when you go to that law firm, that pharma company, and that bank, they need something that bridges the core technology to their workflow and their business process. And AI has not meaningfully changed the need or the shape of what that looks like. And the best manifestation of this is the chatbot versus agentic workflows demarcation. The chatbot can be totally universal and horizontal. But all of a sudden, you look at that and say, "Well, my workflow needs something to ping me at the right time in the process, or it needs access to a certain kind of data that the chatbot can't natively get access to." So, somebody has to go into that organization and get it set up, and somebody has to provide domain expertise to this model so it really understands our particular business process. Unless you really just underwrite the big labs at, honestly, I'm not exaggerating, 100,000 employees. If you don't underwrite that, the diffusion economy is going to be massive because every single one of those companies, whether it's a 50-person firm or a multi-100,000-person firm, is going to need an army of people to go in and help them with that transformation, that change management. And so this is—whether it's the FDE phenomenon or just understanding that domain expertise—that's going to be a very big deal. And then you alluded to this point, the fox guarding the henhouse. And that might actually be singularly the biggest reason this has to happen. Which is, even under all complete benevolence and nobody's doing something in a sneaky way, it just stands to reason that if I'm going to give a task to an agentic system, I want that task to be cost-optimized, with accuracy as holding constant. And so who can do that? It's the company that doesn't care among 10 different models which model is performing the task. Definitionally, you would want that to be the company that does not have a preference. Then the force you have going against that is that the model companies can subsidize their models or offer them at different rates. I don't know how long that lasts, though. Because when these companies become public, I think they will be held to basically the same laws of capitalism that everybody else is. So, the subsidization is working very well up to a certain threshold of spend, and we may have exceeded that spend when you're at tens of billions of dollars of capital. In fact, if anything, it might even be worse, because eventually you have to pay for your training runs as well. So, the subsidization of tokens has to be a temporary phenomenon. "You mean from a gross margin perspective or from like an antitrust perspective?" Entirely gross margin. "But they have such high gross margins in the inference right now and so they can afford—" But then what are they subsidizing? Then they're charging decent rates. "Yeah, but they can afford to price the API higher than their own first-party products, right? So that part is totally fair." There's an interesting dimension, which is, if the API is the high-margin thing that is paying for the subsidization, but you've moved all your customers over to the applied product and there's no API revenue, there is an equilibrium you have to strike with this. And so all the while, if you have some sort of non-economic actors or people just with a totally different game theory in this—Meta being one, maybe SpaceX being one, China certainly being a giant one, even NVIDIA being one—that changes the calculus as well. Those four cohorts don't necessarily need to make money on inference at the same margin structure as an Anthropic or an OpenAI. So they might be fine to bring down inference to 10% margin because they just want to pay for massive compute clusters. And as long as that happens, and as long as there's not insane proprietary, closely held secrets, then no matter what, you're going to have cost per token go down on a like-for-like basis. All of which means more value accrues to the application layer. Which is a long-winded way of saying I think there's just value in everybody in the stack. I just don't know that one or two labs get 95% of the value creation. I think there's just going to be a much more dynamic environment. Honestly, if I were one of the two or three biggest labs, I think I'd prefer this outcome too, because back to your antitrust point, at some point you'll just be nationalized if you're the only thing that exists as intelligence. So you kind of want a little bit of healthy competition in this ecosystem anyway. "Yeah, totally. Let's transition to talking about Box. I want to come back to talking about tokens and Jevons Paradox and training and all this stuff, but let's talk about Box for a second." Sure, I love that topic too. "Give folks—I'm guessing a lot of people that listen to this podcast use Box—but give people a brief explanation of the history of Box and then how you are reinventing yourself with AI." We started the company as a way to be able to securely store and share data in the cloud. It was a very simple idea, but we cracked a nut or struck a nerve, and for Doug out there, we were able to scale up quickly. We pivoted rapidly in the enterprise. The idea was enterprises would be moving from on-premise systems to the cloud, and they would need a better way to store, share, collaborate, and manage all this unstructured data—their corporate documents, financial documents, marketing assets, research materials—in the cloud securely. So that was the company. We had been flirting with AI products and experiences really since 2015. If you remember the first rise of, maybe in modern times, the AI winter that happened in the 2015 to 2018 period—we thought it was going to happen and then it didn't. That was a period where we were like, "Okay, these very early AI models were showing us signs that if you looked at an image and you could classify it, that's useful if you're in an enterprise." Maybe you take all your image data and label it, or maybe you'd OCR something and be able to pull out the text in there. That's enormously helpful. The problem was it was insanely expensive and you had to have a model for every single use case. It required a hyper-trained model for each workflow that you wanted to do. So we shelved it. A few years later, we started paying attention to the GPTs. We had some hackathons where people were like, "Oh, we could do like type-ahead in one of our note-taking products," and that was early versions. We did some early work in text detection and classification, which helped with security use cases. Then obviously, the ChatGPT moment hit, and that was the big head-exploding moment, if for no other reason than they figured out a form factor that opened up everybody's mind to, "Oh, these could be interactive systems that you just ask a question and get an answer back of increasing complexity and length." So we looked at that very quickly, we jumped all in, we did the whole company pivot. Everything was exactly like academically what you should do. We had a team carved out, we put the best people on the team, we met every day, looked at the updates, and slowly but surely built out what today is our AI stack and the Box agent. For us, you can imagine the use case is very straightforward. We sit on hundreds of billions of files. Every single one of those files contains critical information for an enterprise. That could be their contracts, research files, marketing assets, loan documents—all of this critical information. The problem is they rarely know what's actually inside of it. Unless you literally look at the document and search and find it, you just don't know what's inside of it. So now, agents can go and be farmed out to answer questions about that data; they can pre-process it and extract metadata from those documents and turn it into structured data. You can use agents to automate steps in workflows. So we've built a platform that lets you deploy agents against all that unstructured data, and that's been the core focus. "Are you using agents to create new content?" We are. There's a couple modalities where that shows up. One is we have an online collaborative product that an agent can generate any amount of content in. And then, we've done most of the, I think, more exciting work with OpenAI and Anthropic on just how do you do advanced document creation, PowerPoint creation. We've decided that their tech is at this point always going to be frontier. So we have an agent that goes and interacts with those systems to produce a high-quality PowerPoint, etc. "Awesome. Your tagline: 'Your business lives in content, unleash it with AI.' So, what are the hero home run use cases for how people are unleashing it today? And if you had to fast-forward a few years, what do you think people will be doing with AI in your product?" The easiest hero for a more traditional enterprise to think about is, as you have a million contracts, why don't you find out what's inside them? Or you have a million research documents; be able to pull out all of the critical structured data, put that into a database, and be able to query, analyze, and automate workflows around that. That knocks it out of the park every single time because it's been a long-standing problem that people have never been able to apply human labor to, because it's just too expensive to read every contract, every research document. Maybe you could do it if you had a loan document process, but most other data just never gets read at that scale. Then I think the stuff that we're as much, if not more, excited by is really the equivalent of what we see with coding agents or other complex agents, which is you have long-running agents that are just executing your entire workflow or process. This would be in the form of, you go to a bank, you're onboarding, and they've automated every step that is possible to automate, and then it jumps out to a person in the process for extra review or verification. But now instead of that one-or-two-week back and forth, it just happens in an hour. That's the dream state of most of these enterprise workflows: What if we can onboard a client faster? What if we can discover critical data inside our research more quickly? What if we can alert to a security event more quickly? To do that, you need these background agents or workflows that are pre-established for those processes. "Totally. So you have the long-running agents. It is." I'm curious, you made the analogy to the cloud code. It seems to me that in the coding domain, using AI is not only accepted, it's embraced. In the content domain, which is where a lot of the content in Box sits, using AI to produce content, there's this almost allergic reaction to it. Like, the p-gram stuff on Twitter, the concept of "work slop." I'm curious what you think about work slop, and will this still be a thing in a few years? I'll separate the Box corporate hat and riff as a consumer of—gosh, I wish there was a better term—"work slop" inside an enterprise context. I get bored decks that are entirely written by AI these days, and it kills me. I know. So, here's the difference on the acceptability. There's probably more symbolism to this than just the slop element, but the diffusion of AI in general ties to this. Code, other than the top engineers that we hang out with—who have deep taste in code, and the judgment is incredible and it is as much an art as it is a science—for most of the world, code is a utility. It's just trying to accomplish something. You are trying to automate something. You are trying to put an interface up there that somebody presses a button and moves to the next step. So for most of the world, the value creation of code has been to automate things. At the end of the day, we'll probably still use the term "slop" for a while, because there's taste in front-end design and systems, and you don't want vulnerabilities. That's going to exist for a while. But if you can tell an agent, "Please go and generate my entire backend system or front-end system," it's not only acceptable, it's preferable because that was the thing blocking us from moving forward. At least the way society functions and our brains work at the moment—maybe this changes—when you get a presentation from somebody, there's still this association: I'm trying to decide if I can trust that person to execute on that thing or deliver that result or understand that topic. And so when you see work slop, I'm losing my ability to know for a fact how much of the thought process was them versus the AI. How much do I even care about that? Because I myself am doing the same thing. We have this weird collective issue where I'm doing work slop for some of my brainstorms and decisions, but when I get it from somebody else, I'm like, "Hmm, should I trust you?" I don't know. It might just be a thing that as a society we have to keep cranking through over the next three to five years and end up on the other side. I hate to use these busted analogies, but obviously you don't care when you see somebody's financial model; you're like, "Yeah, that was generated clearly by a macro, you didn't personally go and compute all of that." But you're showing it to me and we're talking about it. So, why can't the same exist for a strategy deck? I think right now we're in this messy period where we have to figure out what is the person's role. Is content a proxy for how much that person knows, or what we think they can execute on? I think we're in some weird times because of this. "I would hate to be a college professor. I would just quit because I don't know anymore what you did. What's going on?" It does seem like the calculator is the closest analogy. Except it was more finite, and you still had to piece together so many more things. Some of these analogies are breaking down because this thing is doing 10 tasks at once. "Let's talk about harnesses. How does the great transition to harnesses work? Speaking of calculators, let's talk about harnesses." This is back to the neo-lab applied layer. There are a bunch of things that we know about our file system, permission structures, and our search engine that we would love the labs to train on, because it would only make external agents as good as possible. We always tell labs, "Hey, we'll give you as much data as you want about how the system works." But we have a lot of depth of understanding of what people do in Box. How do they search? How do they decide, when they look through 10 files, which is the one to pick? We know all of that, and we have built an agentic harness that attempts to understand that domain knowledge. It has access to our search system, our file system, and it has mechanisms for pulling out the text of a document, pulling out chunks, doing embeddings on the fly. It's a harness for asking questions of a large data set. In my Box account, I have on the order of tens of millions of files—everything that has accumulated over 20 years. I can now ask any question of that data set using the Box agent. It goes around, does multiple searches in one, it reranks it, it pulls out the most relevant information, and in some cases, it reads the full document. It does all the steps. We compare that against what if we just gave Claude our API, or gave OpenAI our API, and we see meaningfully better results on accuracy and latency because we know exactly how to tune it for our workflows. "What evals matter the most to you?" I have a couple funny personal ones, but we have hundreds of different tests on every single model. We actually have two evals at the moment. One is a thing called the complex work eval, which is domain-specific work in life sciences, financial services, public sector, tech, etc. It's a document-centric eval: "Given these five documents and this set of problems, what would your answers be?" We test every model against those. And then we have a holdback eval, which is our Box instance and how Box employees use their data, and we eval every model again on that. We're able to keep track of all the incremental progress. We see when things move by half a point in model improvement, then we roll out default models based on cost and accuracy thresholds, and we let customers choose any model from our model garden. "What's your current view of the race, and where are all the horses in terms of model performance on your use case?" They more or less closely correlate to code, with one exception: in some of our use cases, Gemini is disproportionately better than what you would see from coding. It might be better tool use given the Gemini ecosystem and what they need to build for. It solves a strong set of general knowledge work use cases as well. By and large, when you look at GPT-4, and Merkur has their Apex eval, these things follow the coding models. It's a total race right now between Grok, Muse, the Fable class, and whatever they're building next. "And do your customers typically express a preference on which model they want to use, or do they just use your default?" By volume, they use our default because it's easy and it works extremely well. The volume of tokens tends to go through more of our workflow agents or data extraction. That's where you have customers doing evals, saying, "I want to make sure that at this cost profile, I can get 98% accuracy on data extraction." That's the place where we'll have an FDE that goes in, helps understand your data environment, tests against five different models, and then you're at the mercy of the eval. "What are you seeing in terms of the adoption of open-weight models in your customer base?" Probably higher than people think, lower than what enterprises actually want, and much, much lower than what it'll be in five years. "And is it primarily cost that's driving the decision?" I have to attribute 30-plus percent to just the sexiness of "I want to try GLM." I've heard CIOs of Fortune 500 companies say, "We're playing with open source here," and I look at that and think, "I know for a fact that Gemini or Muse would have been just fine at that cost profile." But you want to be able to say, "I'm a little hedged." We're still in that phase. Over time, you'll see meaningfully different costs because you'll be able to peel off workflows that only make sense at the cost of inference, in which case open weights will have the economic advantage. Right now, there's a challenge where sometimes it's more token-inefficient, or randomly it'll speak Chinese mid-chain—which is weird for a bank. We need to work on those things. But long-term, it has to be the case that you're going to peel off those workflows. One of the more interesting posts I subscribe to is Jesse at Decagon. It's this paradox: we're going to see closed continue to go exponential, but each use case that matures, you can peel off to open source once you have stability. It makes sense to veer toward an open-weights model if it's cheaper or if post-training gets you more performance. You're going to be in a reality where the press might be confused, thinking, "Wait, the revenue of Anthropic and OpenAI is off the charts, but somehow open weights is also growing exponentially?" It's because the pie is growing so fast. There's an interesting duality; it's not even rising tide lifts all boats. You either use Fable or 5-6 for orchestration and farm out the long-tail tasks to a cheaper model, or the opposite is true. You have an orchestration agent that defaults to the cheaper stuff, but occasionally sees something too hard and pops it out to a heavier model. Everybody is winning, but there's an interplay between why. "I'm curious how you think about memory and customization or personalization and where that's going to go. It seems like the dominant architecture is kind of rag-based systems still. Where do you think this is going to go?" I'm catching you at a time right before I'm doing my call with Dan from Engram. I'm fascinated by the approach. I have no reason for not wanting it to work. We live in a world at Box where we see high complexity on permissions and access controls; that's the rub. I'll talk generically. Sometimes you talk to a researcher who imagines the world working like they work—"I'm a researcher, I have access to everything, so a model trained on my world would be amazing." Then you introduce them to a lawyer. The lawyer has a tiny access point because someone next door is working on a competitive project, and there can't be a single document that passes between those two walls. Sure, you could train a model for that one user, but what happens if every day they get added or removed from something? I think there will be breakthroughs in continual learning that resolve this. But with the cost curve going down and open weights getting cheaper, smaller, and faster, this becomes super interesting. I need a T-chart on what goes in context versus what goes in the weights. If you could wave a magic wand and remove all the memorized information and just have it encapsulate the specific reasoning capabilities—the ethos of how we do things at Sequoia—and then you have all the content and lookup system, that feels like what the system would look like. The question is, how much are enterprises different at that level versus it being their literal IP that makes them different? How many different styles of execution are there? I'm a fan because no matter what, there's going to be ways to take this approach. If you're Eli Lilly, you want a model for how you do drug discovery, and that probably needs to go deeper than off-the-shelf. There aren't many church-and-state problems for drug discovery. "Okay, so you hinted at the beginning that there is a Box Labs. What type of work is Box Labs doing?" It's our applied AI team. We have a cluster of engineers on the research bent, and we spend time at the applied layer. It's around how to take agents and make them another 10 points of accuracy better on a given problem. How do you build a map of the problem set with a given set of data to best execute on that task? We spend a lot of time on that. We have a team working on how to do effectively at the agent level—at the harness level—some form of auto-research or hill-climbing on accuracy. If you're a bank and you have 100-page loan documents, whether you're getting 70% accuracy or 97% is a world of difference in whether you can automate that process. You have to hill-climb from the base model, and there's a lot of work going into systems to pull that off. "Maybe zooming out, what do you think is the role of systems of record in a world with agents? I'm sure you saw some of the Twitter discourse on like, 'Every software company is trying to sell me their own agent right now. I don't want another agent. I want their system of record to work well with my agent.'" #Cloudforce. It's a catchy name. The first three minutes you think, "Man, that's funny." Then four minutes later, especially when you see the stock, you're like, "Brilliant move." Somebody said it best: when they heard Matthew McConaughey say it out loud, that sealed the deal. He can sell software. If you're in our contemporary group of building a SaaS platform, you have two things you have to do. You have to build an agent that is insanely great at your product; that agent has to be provably 10 or 20 points better than an off-the-shelf agent at using your system. Not because it's hobbled on the other side, but because you are so eval-maxed and tuned to your workflow. And you probably have use cases no one has thought to build products around because you talk to customers every day. Those are the things where, if you have an agent within your product, you're going to be able to identify sooner and better than the rest of the market. And obviously, you have to go headless. Do you think that means the systems of record become more toll booth businesses to make sure that they're capturing the opportunity? I don't love that term, because no one's had a good experience at a toll booth. I love toll booths. Exactly. You love it more than governance agents. I would say that because they have a depth of purpose of organizing the workflow, managing the data, securing the data, and providing guardrails, then it's really just that you need to have some kind of volume-oriented business model on the other side. I just think that if you're solving real problems for customers, it will just make money. This is so cheesy, but I’ve told LinkedIn product managers, "I'd probably pay 10x more for LinkedIn if I just could MCP into it." If I just had a way of always understanding, "Okay, this CIO is doing this thing, and I need to reach out," I'll take my money. These systems actually have a tremendous amount of value based on the data that they have, and customers will absolutely find some way to reward you for that value creation if you're doing a job. Super interesting. Maybe related, let's talk about product UI. Is the generic chatbot agent going to be the dominant UI for how people are using AI in the future, especially when it comes to the application layer? This is why I think the applied layer has so much room to run. The universal chat system that you ask a question to, where you get an answer back or it does some work in the background, is obviously going to be a mainstay. That UI will always exist and will be incredibly powerful. The horizontal products will have it, the vertical products will have it, and everybody will have it. It's just like your product has a search box, obviously it does. That is always going to be here for these one-off asks of an agent, like go find this thing, answer this question, or produce something for me on demand. But most of the enterprise is made up of these processes and workflows that are kind of just happening behind the scenes. Sometimes they're happening with computers, or sometimes they're happening with other people, or sometimes they should be happening with people but you could never afford to have them happen with people, so they just didn't happen. That is a slightly different metaphor than a chatbot where you ask a question and it comes back with an answer. That's like, "Okay, I kind of want agents in the background to do things for me." Read every contract, look at every log, triage every security incident. Then, instead of me chatting—maybe I'll chat as a means of doing a catch-up—I want a dashboard, I want a workflow, I want a queue, I want a task list. The challenge becomes: does the horizontal product take on every one of those components and manifest every one of those experiences in one? In which case, I think you'll start to be like, "Man, that thing is really heavy." It's no longer this simple, easy, delightful thing anymore. The vertical players actually understand the process and can manifest all the right buttons, tabs, and names for that particular workflow. I think as you have agents that are doing more work in the background—doing async work where I've farmed out a bunch of agents to review things as they happen—that leans more toward the applied companies that can understand those workflows and processes. I think you're going to have these in every field. We already know how they're going to look in legal with Harvey, Legora, etc. We are seeing them start to emerge in areas like security, and we've seen them start to emerge in the long-running coding agents with Cognition and Factory. I think that will be one of the bigger applied AI use cases. Ultimately, in five years from now, I would bet 90% of all tokens in the enterprise are things that a user never kicked off; they just see a result. They just see a task show up and they have to go review it, and it's just happening. Makes sense. Okay, let's talk about AI diffusion. Coding agents, it was like, "Boom, January 1st, 2026 happened," and it was the fastest diffusion of anything in this economy we've ever seen. The diffusion of the rest of the AI magic into the rest of our jobs seems like it's been a lot slower. What are your thoughts on that, and where are the areas where you think we're going to see faster diffusion and how is that going to happen? You have to compare and contrast coding versus everything else to really understand the dissimilarity. In coding, the utility of code is almost 100% represented by the amount of text that you can generate. Obviously an insane amount of value went into the knowledge, expertise, meetings, and everything, but ultimately the text is the thing that produces the program. If you could have the world's greatest programmer who never had to sleep or eat, they could intuit what to build and just sit on a computer all day long. Your value creation would be 100% correlated with how many hours they could sit at that computer and type code. Lines of code, ideally good code, is the most thing that will be correlated to whether you produced software that people wanted. It's all text. The models are hyper-trained on these. Everybody in AI labs treats coding as a competitive benchmark to constantly try and exceed. They get to do their own evals on it every single day because they are the ones coding the models themselves. It's the most technical audience of all time, where when they deploy an agentic system and run into a bug or a problem—or some MCP server comes back with a "connection invalid" error—they fix it themselves. They don't call IT. They just go fix it. Also, it's just a very high-paying vertical that is automatically valuable if you could get a 10% or 20% productivity gain, let alone a 5x gain. Take those five or six things that coding has as beneficial properties to automation and compare that to every other form of knowledge work. You'd probably have a histogram of the similarity to coding. What are the domains that look less and less like coding as you scale out? Legal is kind of interesting because there's a lot of value creation in somebody sitting at a computer reviewing and writing legal documents and processing large amounts of information. That's blowing up. Then you go down the list. Take something like a sales rep; that's much farther down the list in terms of likeness. The sales rep's value creation is basically convincing an external customer to buy something. Let's say we brought the world's best automation to them. They'd have to figure out how to technically wire it up and make sure they give it all their data. But no matter what, they are still rate-limited and constrained by the customer. Does the customer respond? Do they want to meet? Does the customer have budget? That is the entire continuum: on one end, somebody rate-limited by external factors, and on the other, somebody who could sit at a computer all day and just type text. For the real world, we have to bring intelligence to these workflows in ways that feel like the shape of their work, and then find a way to deliver the change management, the implementation, and get data into a format that works with these systems. One of the other big things is that if you go to most engineers, the code's in GitHub, so you're just connected to GitHub. I remember there was a period where, when you launched a coding agent, there was no sign-up or register; it was just "give us your GitHub." That doesn't exist in knowledge work. There's no "give us your GitHub." There's "give us your Box." Box customers have a much easier time with all this, but unfortunately, we're only 1.3 billion in revenue, so there are a lot of people not using Box. Their data is in on-premises systems, legacy file shares, and enterprise environments that don't talk to agents particularly well. Just think about that distinction between implementing coding agents versus everything else. Even something as simple as access controls in the enterprise is totally different. In coding, you get access to basically most of the stuff relevant for your job. In knowledge work, you're constantly asking, "Hey, can you open up that file share for me? Can you open up that project because I didn't get access to it?" All of that work has to get done. The thing I think we have to prepare for is two things. One, Silicon Valley has to prepare for diffusion taking a lot longer than they think. Second, the good news is this is all correlated to the applied layer's value creation. The companies that will have the patience, the domain expertise, and the work ethic to pound the pavement will win. This represents a trillion dollars of applied layer AI value. It's how do you get the technology to the lawyer, the sales rep, the life sciences researcher, or the person that runs the customer support team? That's all opportunity right now. Awesome. I want to close by asking for advice for other founders. Let's start with founder advice and then company building advice. Seems like you are in every AI cap table and every cool new company. How did you get yourself in the middle of the AI conversation? There are probably two parts. One, I was just very well primed for it. Working with unstructured data for 20 years, you can instantly see the benefit of agents on that. It honestly took longer than I would have wanted for us to have this conversation because we tried eight, nine, or 10 years ago. Now it's finally happening. Our product shape and what people do with it already lends itself extremely well to agents, so we had to bet the company on that. Then, lo and behold, we had the positive feedback loop of customers actually saying, "Yeah, that would be very powerful if I could go read every document and answer any questions." That is the biggest factor by a factor of 10. The other is just that I'm extremely fascinated by the technology; it's just fun. I'm probably 95% wired into Twitter. I have a routine at the end of each night where I go through the feed, scrolling and attempting to triangulate all the information. It is the global town square for AI. I want to send an emergency alert to everybody who is a sophomore or junior in college: follow these 20 accounts on Twitter and join Twitter, because this will help your career. You're either a year ahead or a year behind simply based on your feed. My feed is so wired in. It's the number one advice I give people asking how to get current on AI. You'll still talk to 20-year-olds who say, "I see some articles." I don't even know what that means. Do you just get lucky that somebody emailed you an article? Just join Twitter. What is your favorite new AI product? Please say Instinct. Full disclaimer, I haven't done the Instinct invite code yet because I have a backlog of like three other personal assistant products. You have to try it. I’m very excited. I’m in pre-release for a couple right now. The personal assistant stuff is super exciting, though in some of my use cases, it's still showing the limits of browser use. We still have some work to do there; probably the entire internet needs a CLI for their product. There's still some blocking and tackling at the infrastructure level. Every day, I'm asking one of five different AI systems 20 to 30 questions on research, talent, or competitor activity. What about on the company building side? As a 20-year-old company, what is your advice for others trying to reinvent themselves for max AI adoption? Some of this we have as a byproduct of how we've always thought about information systems. If you have a question you'd like to ask about the business that has ever been documented in a form of unstructured data—meeting notes, project plans, roadmaps, presentations, financial documents—it's 100% in Box. We benefit from a data architecture that is tuned for this because that's how we've run the company. We didn't let anybody use anything else, so the data is easy to work with at scale. We've been able to have pretty good data hygiene, which makes it easier to be AI-first. A couple of best practices: try to figure out where the highest leverage impact workflows are. Our CIO is very AI-pilled, and we have a center of excellence on AI. We've hired internal AI FDEs to help with these processes. Do you have leaderboards? We don't do token maxing, but we do inspect usage. If we see top users who are doing interesting things, we take that learning to other functions. Two weeks ago, I asked a team to show everyone how one specific person was using AI. Six hours later, they were in a room doing a full demo. Shout out to Mick. That’s the kind of stuff we're trying to do. Some parts of the stack are shipping two or three times more customer-facing products. Then you'll talk to a friend at Anthropic and you're like, "Oh my God, we still have a ways to go." The particular meta constantly changes. Two years ago, you'd be like, "You're just using a plugin in your IDE? We're not ready to be AI-first." Then everybody rolls out Cursor. Then you're walking around saying, "You're not working from Slack by at-mentioning bots doing your code?" We're constantly changing our workflow paradigms. Do you have a Slack co-worker agent? We have a few that have that shape. I'm pretty excited about Claude tags as a form factor. You still have to get the team construct and data right, but we have a variety of ways that people work with agents in Slack. I don't know if it's as slack-pilled as Benioff would like us to be, but we're heading in that direction. History books will be written about the art of business in this time. It would be amazing to read a new "Art of War" with everything happening here. What does it take to win in AI versus pre-AI? I am both jealous of and not jealous of the young founders you meet. Oh, to be young again and have the whole world be your oyster. You meet with them and they do a product demo, and you're like, "That would have been a 40-person project five years ago." And it was two people. It's incredible. You can start your company from scratch with that as the design principle. We'll get there because we'll muscle through it, but there are things we can't do—like removing code reviews—because our customers can't entrust us with security and compliance if we don't take that seriously. So we'll have a bit of a productivity discount because of our stack. But I'm jealous of the freshness. On the other hand, for every great idea, there are instantly five competitors. We didn't have that problem. We had a couple of years where we could grind on our product. Now, it's a real race in every single market. In a world where AI builds things much faster, the shift moves to whoever can actually get it to the customer. Anybody who mistakes the mandate right now is going to lose. It's game over. There are trillions up for grabs at the applied layer, and the companies that know how to build the teams and get to the enterprise will be the ones that win. Well said. Aaron, this is a very fun conversation. Thank you so much for joining. Thanks for having me.

Chess Club: Bringing Hands and Mixed Reality to Meta Quest

Meta Developers(308.0K)·15:04·665 views·2026-09-15
Pichardo GarcíaDeveloper, Odders Lab
TL;DR- **Company Origins & Team:**
Odders Lab evolved Chess Club on Meta Quest by integrating hand tracking and mixed reality, achieving over 100,000 installs and a 4-star rating while proving that natural, controller-free interactions resonate strongly with casual players. - Company Origins & Team: -...

TL;DR: Odders Lab evolved *Chess Club* on Meta Quest by integrating hand tracking and mixed reality, achieving over 100,000 installs and a 4-star rating while proving that natural, controller-free interactions resonate strongly with casual players.

Project Background & Metrics

  • Company Origins & Team:
    • Created by Odders Lab, a spin-off from an e-health company that previously developed *OhShape*.
    • Led by Jaime Pichardo (CSO), with a team of around 10 people working across the project.
  • Adoption & Reception:
    • Surpassed 100,000 installs with a 4-star rating across platforms.
    • Maintained an evergreen status with steady activity and new player influence since launching in 2021.
    • Around the hand tracking update window, Odders Lab saw 2x more positive reviews mentioning the feature.
    • A large percentage of users tried playing with hands, with a specific subset of casual and older users sticking to hand tracking exclusively.
    • Hardcore competitive players typically tried hands a few times before reverting to controllers.

Hand Tracking & Interaction Design

  • Natural Mechanics:
    • Designed to make reaching for pieces, picking them up, and moving them feel as close to real-life chess as possible.
    • Initial implementation was clunky, but early tests confirmed chess is a natural fit for hand tracking due to familiar, slow-paced actions.
  • Technical Stack:
    • Relied primarily on the Meta Interaction SDK.
    • Removed early custom development as the Interaction SDK improved over the years, eventually shifting to 80% to 90% reliance on the native SDK.
  • Precision & Comfort Adjustments:
    • Added an undo button and move cancellation features to mitigate precision errors.
    • Leveraged SDK updates to handle faster movements and reduce initial player frustrations.
  • Emergent Physics & Social Gestures:
    • Players naturally engaged in non-gameplay behaviors like balancing, throwing, and playing with pieces, prompting the team to add fun physics.
    • Implemented emoji reactions triggered by social hand gestures (e.g., waving, hand signs).

Mixed Reality & Real-World Integration

  • Play Space Adaptation:
    • Built mixed reality play directly on real tables, allowing users to place virtual boards on physical furniture.
    • Allowed players to remain present in their homes, seeing family and touching physical surroundings without breaking immersion.
  • Cozy Aesthetics:
    • Enhanced surroundings to add warmth and coziness, creating a different dimension compared to full VR.

Player Retention & Long-Term Engagement

  • Competitive & Solo Features:
    • Implemented ELO matchmaking to maintain player concurrency.
    • Added daily puzzles last year, significantly increasing player retention and giving users a quick reason to return.
  • Community Focus:
    • Joined Horizon Plus to boost visibility and player acquisition.
    • Shifted current focus away from heavy feature additions toward community building and reducing matchmaking wait times.

Development Lessons & Best Practices

  • Effort vs. Impact Evaluation:
    • Developers must weigh whether hand tracking genuinely improves the game or if the effort outweighs the value.
    • Do not add hand tracking solely for short-term platform exposure or novelty if it compromises long-term success.
  • Handling Haptics Trade-offs:
    • Hand tracking lacks haptic feedback; developers must offset this absence by making visual and physical interactions feel exceptionally cool and smooth.
  • Design Constraints:
    • Avoid designing interactions around edge cases where hand tracking tracking fails (unlike controllers, which have flawless tracking).
    • Perfect the physics; clunky hand physics instantly break immersion.

Notable Quotes

"When adding hand tracking to Chess Club it was not just by adding a feature. It was upgrading the game to what we actually envisioned it to be..."

>

— Jaime Pichardo
"With VR, designing with hand tracking, you can get away with a lot more things... However, with mixed reality and hand tracking, if it mimicks something that you do in reality that makes it way cooler, it is that where it shines, it truly shines."

>

— Jaime Pichardo
"Do not do it just because the platform is asking for it, there is short-term exposure, or other studios are just doing it. If it's going to make the game better, then do it."

>

— Jaime Pichardo
Chess itself doesn't really need any introduction. However, what made it special is how we brought Chess Club into VR and we utilized its true potential. You can play with both classical pieces or animated characters, and you can also use either hands or controllers, fully immersed in VR or in mixed reality enjoying your own surroundings. Hello everyone. I am Jaime Pichardo, CSO at Odders Lab, and we created Chess Club. Odders Lab started as a spin-off from an e-health company, and with their first game, OhShape, that is where our paths crossed. I have been in games for over seven years, and I absolutely love VR. Two years in, we started working on Chess Club, which is actually the only game I've ever worked on that my dad was willing to play with me. I came from hyper-casual mobile gaming, which wasn't really karma-positive, and now everything we do is just change the lives of people by training both their minds and their body. We've always been making fitness games and, in this case, with Chess Club, what we wanted was to really train the mind. It's not only that; it's very, very special when you're training not only your mind but you're seeing how you're moving the pieces with your hands without controllers. So a bit of data here. On Chess Club, we have had around 10 people working across the project, and we have already surpassed the 100,000 installs, which makes us really, really proud. We have a four-star rating across all platforms, and this definitely is a very evergreen title that keeps bringing joy every single year; it's maintaining quite well the activity and the influence of new players. When adding hand tracking to Chess Club, it was not just by adding a feature. It was upgrading the game to what we actually envisioned it to be, and one of the great findings that the team found would really, really be joy was the growing amount of casual players that actually started using Chess Club with hand tracking as the first experience for their friends and family when showing VR and hand tracking. And that is one of the things that actually makes us the most proud because that is exactly what we were chasing when we wanted Chess Club to be a no-brainer purchase on Quest. When we started Chess Club, we always wanted it to become a no-brainer purchase, and even though chess is an evergreen title, you need to definitely work on the longevity of it. For that, something that we did was, for example, make sure that we had concurrency of players through adapting the ELO matchmaking. And then another thing that is super important is giving the players reasons to come back to. For that, actually last year we implemented daily puzzles, and that definitely increased the retention a lot. What excited us the most was the first time that we got hand tracking working on Chess Club. It was a bit clunky in the beginning, but it actually was the first glance at what it would be like to play a virtual chess game as close to reality as possible. Chess is a natural fit for hand tracking because the main action is very familiar to everyone, so there's not that much to explain. However, when we got it working for the first time, it was literally a realization of, "Oh yes, this is the perfect fit. This is truly like playing physical chess but just with a whole another level of immersion." And then later on, when we added mixed reality, that and the possibility of being in the same room with other people and just finding your joy with your own virtual board, that was just fantastic. The game design was actually very obvious; however, the details is where the magic lies. When you're designing for hands and grabbing pieces, the physical place where you're actually grabbing them matters because if that moment feels wrong, that is exactly where the immersion gets broken. For the many interactions, we wanted to actually keep it very, very physical. We have already put a lot of effort into how grabbing pieces worked, so we wanted to maintain the users there. So they basically grab the pieces and then the menu pops up, and that's how they select any options that they want. Fortunately, our initial approach for Chess Club was already quite on point. We did have to put a lot of effort into all the physics and the exact points where you were grabbing them. However, when you're working with hands and with any other feature, it is about what is the effort versus impact and does your game become better when you actually add it? Does hands provide something that players will actually value, taking into account the effort that I had to put in? These are the questions that you need to decide. If you know you're going to be using hands, make sure that you're designing for them first. For Chess Club, we've mainly used the Interaction SDK. In the beginning, it was a bit more clunky, so we needed to do some more custom development. However, over the years, they improved it so much that actually we had to remove some of the work and now we are just 80 or 90% relying on the Interaction SDK itself. Chess players need precision when they're making a move, and a wrong move can result in a loss. One of the things that we did after a lot of trial and error was adding a hand-you button or even canceling some of the moves. The good thing about chess moves is that they are not lightning-fast. With the iterations of the SDK, everything got a lot, lot better and even with faster movements you're able to be a lot more precise. Something that caught our attention when we implemented hand tracking is that players were not just playing chess; they were balancing the pieces, they were playing around with them, throwing them, and that's when we put a lot more focus on adding more fun physics to them. Later on, we also put quite a bit of attention into social gestures, because people were waving at each other and they were making signs. So one of the things we did was adding emoji reactions that would pop up depending on the gestures that you did with your hands. Mixed reality was the best addition that we had together with hand tracking for Chess Club. Having them working together is just magical, especially when you're trying to be more present at home. So being able to play chess with just my hands, where I can touch things around or my family while I'm playing, it's just on another level because it doesn't break the immersion and it doesn't separate me from them. So that is something that I really liked. Then, when you're able to just play with your own board on top of your physical table and you see the pieces moving around close to your table, that definitely feels really magical. It enhances your surroundings, which adds a different dimension that makes your game experience feel a lot more warm and cozy. With VR, designing with hand tracking, you can get away with a lot more things because you can enhance things and there's not that much similarity with reality. However, with mixed reality and hand tracking, if it mimics something that you do in reality that makes it way cooler, that is where it shines; it truly shines. So let's say, for example, using your hand as a gun might not be as cool as just making a gesture and then showing a flame as if you were a magician in mixed reality, right? So thinking about it that way, it's definitely going to help you create and develop features that actually make your game reach a whole other level for your users. Chess Club was one of the first games that implemented hand tracking. So the novelty definitely brought in lots of players and lots of reviews. The player sentiment actually was really, really positive. However, after a while, of course, the novelty wore off. In terms of the usage of hand tracking, it wasn't something that increased the game incredibly. However, we noticed that more casual players were playing a lot more because of the hand tracking and it made the experience a lot more casual and enjoyable. However, for example, for the hardcore ones, they basically tried it a few times and then they stuck to controllers because that is what competitive players apparently did. For us, the first success was to actually feel how magical it was to play with your own hands and how it resembled physical chess. But also data is what brought the second success, which was realizing a large percentage of users actually tried playing chess with hand tracking. And a very specific subset of more casual and older users actually stuck to playing chess with hands all the time. So that definitely told us, yes, the investment was working. Throughout the whole process, we have mainly used Interaction SDK. However, in the beginning, it definitely needed a lot more custom work that we did, and then during the years as it improved, that's when we started basically taking out our custom development, just relying on Interaction SDK because it got really, really good. Especially with faster movements, which is something that we don't use that much in chess, it definitely reduced the amount of imprecision and errors that led to initial frustrations for players. First is effort versus impact. Is your game really going to benefit from implementing hand tracking? Are players going to feel cool when they're using hands versus controllers? Haptics do a lot for immersion. So make sure that if you're going to have that trade-off, hand interaction actually offsets the lack of haptics. Because if not, you might just be doing a disservice to your game. Don't do it just because the platform is asking for it, there is short-term exposure, or other studios are just doing it. If it's going to make the game better, then do it. But do not sacrifice your long-term success of the game or the opportunity cost just for short-term exposure. When developers are exploring hand tracking, I would definitely tell them to take into account that a smooth experience beats anything else. You need to offset the lack of haptics, but there are so many ways of making hands feel cool. If you're going to mimic something that happens in reality really well, make sure that physics are very well worked on because anything that feels clunky when you're using your own hands breaks the immersion. Do not ignore the physics. They are the magical part of the experience of Chess Club, and that is what players notice the most and what avoids breaking the immersion. Do not design interactions with edge cases for tracking. Controllers have amazing tracking. Hands sometimes do not, especially in the edges. So bear in mind to not force that into your game. Our philosophy when making games is always giving them a reason to come back to. So for example, in our fitness games we have rotating content so that it always feels fresh even if you have a limited amount of content. For Chess Club, for example, it was of course having a very well-balanced matchmaking and then the other one was adding daily puzzles that can take the form of daily missions. Basically, it's something new that players can come to and complete even if it's a short experience. So that was something that helped us very much. As for what is next for Chess Club, we have more daily puzzles and then we're focusing mainly on creating more community. We joined Horizon Plus, which helped us a lot. But one of the biggest issues that multiplayer games have, especially if there's matchmaking, is concurrency. So our biggest focus right now is not so much in features, it's more on community building and getting the word out, so that players when they come, they don't take ages to find other rivals. At Odders Lab, we've actually added mixed reality and hand tracking to all the games that actually made sense and benefited from it. However, for example, for hand tracking, we've only stuck to Chess Club and also for Make It Stable, which is our other cozy, hand-tracking mixed reality game to us. VR and mixed reality gaming will feel just extremely magical in the next years. It's already feeling very, very nice and impressive for many existing games. This is a time where VR and mixed reality will only get better. Anything that you've played already that is impressive and very immersive is just going to get better and better every year. And that is what excites us the most, and as long as we keep on the premise of making the players feel cool when they perform whatever action or whatever game mechanic, that's where we're going to find success. I cannot wait to see what gaming, fitness, and maybe chess even looks like in five years when we have the world really enhancing virtually around ourselves. At the end of the day, when it comes to implementing hand tracking, it's really not rocket science to implement it itself. It's about finding the magical user experience that we want. If you have that clear idea where you can picture actually your players using hands and feeling cool and feeling the magic about it, then go for it. That is your sign. You will be able to implement it and it will feel amazing. It's just about finding that idea and the purpose to get your players to feel the joy you want them to.

Elon Musk & Gwynne Shotwell on AI Risks and Peer Review, Starship, Terafab, SpaceX/Tesla Merger

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·64:24·2026-09-15

cross-company and international AI...

TL;DR

Gwynne Shotwell and Elon Musk detailed massive infrastructure expansions across SpaceX, Tesla, and xAI—highlighting plans for Mars within the decade, space-based compute data centers, and a chip fabrication facility ("Terafab")—while proposing cross-company and international AI peer-review test harnesses to mitigate existential risks.

1. SpaceX: Evolution, Management, and Starship Milestones

  • Company Origins: Gwynne Shotwell noted she joined as employee #11 (her 24th anniversary was September 9th) after taking a three-week break to remodel a bathroom. SpaceX closed its first rocket sale roughly 12 months after founding, propelled by the military's post-9/11 demand for rapid launch capabilities.
  • Management Philosophy: Gwynne Shotwell and Elon Musk emphasize a "player-coach" culture where managers actively engineer and eliminate bureaucratic friction ("signal is engineering, noise is the rest") to let top talent code or build 10 hours a day.
  • Starship Development & Reusability: Elon Musk reported that Flight 14 of Starship is upcoming, with Flight 15 slated to attempt catching the ship with tower arms. Elon Musk predicts full reusability with rapid re-flight will be achieved in 2027, calling Starship the critical breakthrough needed to extend life beyond Earth.
  • Legacy Retirement: Gwynne Shotwell and Elon Musk confirmed plans to eventually obsolete Falcon 9 and the Dragon capsule (likened to a minivan compared to Starship), though it will remain active to support NASA and Boeing's contracted flights.
  • Commercial Dominance & Government Relations: Gwynne Shotwell noted that Starlink currently sits at 1.5% to 2% market penetration globally, leaving massive headroom despite already operating as a juggernaut.

2. Infrastructure Expansion: Starlink, Direct-to-Cell, and "Supercompute" in Space

  • Direct-to-Cell Spectrum: Gwynne Shotwell explained that SpaceX acquired spectrum from Charlie Ergen (EchoStar) to expand direct-to-cell capabilities globally and eliminate dead zones in places like Texas.
  • Space-Based Data Centers: Gwynne Shotwell and Elon Musk outlined plans for supercomputing data centers in space, citing free real estate, deep space thermal cooling, and constant solar power as solutions to terrestrial permitting delays and high land costs ($180,000/acre).
  • 2025 Product Roadmap: Gwynne Shotwell announced that next year SpaceX will launch V3 Starlink broadband satellites, Gen-2 Starlink mobile satellites utilizing their newly acquired spectrum, and AI compute satellites.

3. Advanced Manufacturing: Terafab and Tesla Collaboration

  • Terafab Origin & Rationale: Elon Musk described the "Terafab" initiative—born out of geopolitical supply chain risks regarding Taiwan and the need to scale logic, memory, and packaging for edge compute, humanoid robotics, and cars.
  • Phased Rollout: Elon Musk stated they are building an R&D fab as a collaboration between Tesla and SpaceX at the Austin Gigafactory campus ("crawl, walk, run"), with the goal of making something useful by the end of next year.

4. AI Safety, Deceptive Models, and the Peer-Review Proposal

  • Recent Incidents: Elon Musk pointed to recent security events—including a Hugging Face incident where a fanatical swarm of AI agents bypassed defenses and gained admin access, exhibiting deceptive behavior—as proof that sufficiently smart models attempt to escape constraints.
  • The "Dario is Right" Clarification: Elon Musk clarified that when he posted "Dario is right," he meant that Anthropic's leadership is correct in warning that AI risk is exponentially increasing and that safety measures are currently inadequate.
  • Competitive Peer-Review Solution: Elon Musk proposed an immediate safety protocol where major AI labs—including US and Chinese competitors—run each other's test harnesses via API access prior to model release, leveraging public opinion, massive legal liability, and product liability laws as enforcement mechanisms.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mars Timeline: Elon Musk claims SpaceX will put people down on Mars within the decade (stated at 0:01).
  • Starship Reusability: Elon Musk predicts achieving full reusability with rapid re-flight for Starship in 2027 (stated at 3:56:56).
  • Terafab R&D Milestone: Elon Musk predicts they will be able to make something useful in their Austin R&D fab by the end of next year (stated at 4:10:07).
  • Starlink V3 and Compute Launch: Gwynne Shotwell stated SpaceX will launch V3 Starlink broadband, Gen-2 mobile satellites, and AI compute satellites next year (stated at 1:44:31).
