Extended interview: Trump AI adviser David Sacks on tech leaders' AI fears

CBS News(7.1M)·33:31·2026-09-15
David SacksAI Adviser to President Trump Jo Ling KentCorrespondent, CBS News Dario AmodeiCEO, Anthropic
TL;DRDavid Sacks, presidential AI adviser, dismisses fears of AI-induced human extinction as an overblown political panic, arguing that frontier AI labs must take responsibility for product safety rather than seeking antitrust waivers or halting…
development, which would hand a...

TL;DR

David Sacks, presidential AI adviser, dismisses fears of AI-induced human extinction as an overblown political panic, arguing that frontier AI labs must take responsibility for product safety rather than seeking antitrust waivers or halting development, which would hand a decisive advantage to China.

The AI Extinction Debate & Political Panics

  • Extinction Threat Dismissed as Dramatic: Sacks rejects predictions that AI will end humanity, calling them overly dramatic and part of an amplified election-season panic.
  • Critique of Lab Researchers: Sacks notes that researchers giving high probability numbers (such as a >10% chance of extinction) are corporate employees offering opinions rather than scientific predictions backed by empirical evidence.
  • Political Exploitation: Sacks highlights that political actors and groups are whipping up fear to shape state and federal regulations, noting that coordinated media campaigns—such as the *Wall Street Journal* possessing whistleblower Jacob Coxin's tweet storm 18 minutes before posting—helped drive the recent panic.

Responsibility of Frontier AI Labs

  • The Duopoly Reality: Sacks points out that Anthropic and OpenAI form a "frontier duopoly" controlling model capabilities, revenue, and growth, meaning their leaders (Dario Amodei and Sam Altman) have the direct power to pace the frontier themselves.
  • Contradictory Warnings from CEOs: Sacks questions the logic of Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei warning that frontier AI is dangerous enough to end humanity while continuing to build it, stating that if they cannot control it, they should not do it or should step aside.
  • Support for Product Reliability: Sacks supports labs prioritizing product predictability, robustness, and safety because enterprises do not want models that leak data or hack competitors.
  • Audits and Transparency: Sacks favors independent evaluations, transparency, and safety best-practice sharing among labs, though he questions whether Anthropic's evaluator is a cozy arrangement funded by its own investors.

Regulatory Capture and Legal Liabilities

  • Opposition to Antitrust Waivers: Sacks accuses frontier labs of engaging in "regulatory capture," arguing that Amodei's request for antitrust waivers to pursue AI safety is an attempt to form a cartel and gain protection from competition.
  • Existing Legal Thicket: Citing former Democratic FTC Chair Lina Khan, Sacks notes that AI companies are already subject to extensive existing laws, statutes, and product liability, facing civil lawsuits or criminal liability for unsafe outputs.
  • Rejection of Special Treatment: Sacks firmly opposes granting liability waivers or special government protection to AI companies, asserting they have a standard corporate obligation to make products safe.

The Global AI Race and China

  • Current U.S. Advantages: Sacks assesses that the U.S. maintains a lead over China, putting China about a couple of years behind on chips and roughly 6 to 12 months behind on models.
  • Risks of the "Bernie Sanders Plan": Sacks warns that proposals like Senator Bernie Sanders' bill to pause all AI development would hand the AI race to China, whose pragmatic approach views AI as an economic and national benefit without "doomer" hand-wringing.
  • Impracticability of Global Governance: Sacks dismisses Amodei's proposal for a transnational organization or a deal with China, arguing it would take years to set up, contradicts Amodei's own hostile rhetoric labeling China "evil dictators," and would only slow the U.S. down.
  • Stakes of Losing: Sacks warns that if the U.S. loses the AI race to China, the U.S. will lose its status as the world's number one nation, impacting both the domestic economy and vital military/dual-use capabilities.

Notable Quotes

"If the prediction is that AI is going to end humanity, I think that's uh overly dramatic and I I don't personally uh subscribe to that. I think this is becoming a panic." — David Sacks
"You have the leader of a Frontier AI lab say that Frontier AI is dangerous, so dangerous that it can end humanity. I guess my question when I hear something like that is why are you doing it? ... If you can't control it, then don't do it would be my view. Or step aside uh for people who can." — David Sacks
"If we do the Bernie Sanders plan we will hand the AI race to China and they will benefit from this technology." — David Sacks
David Sacks, thank you so much for doing this. Thanks for having me. You are the co-chair of the president's Council on Science and Technology. Is that right? Yes. Okay, great. People are feeling kind of scared right now about what AI could do to humanity. You've got whistleblowers, the heads of these big AI companies all speaking out. Do you agree with their prediction? If the prediction is that AI is going to end humanity, I think that's overly dramatic, and I don't personally subscribe to that. I think this is becoming a panic. We're in an election season and there is an incentive for political actors to try and amplify the fear. I think this is a moment to really calm down a little bit and look at the issue rationally. I think that AI safety is an issue as the leaders of these labs have said, but it's something that I think we can address and it's something that these labs themselves have to get right. Dario Amodei, the CEO of Anthropic, has said that he agrees with what one of the whistleblowers has to say, that this could all be over. I mean, do you not—these are pretty smart guys at these companies who know this technology saying this stuff too? Well, to me, that's a little bit of a contradiction when I hear something like that. I mean, you have the leader of a Frontier AI lab say that Frontier AI is dangerous, so dangerous that it can end humanity. I guess my question when I hear something like that is, why are you doing it? I mean, you're the leader of this company. Why are you acting like this is something that you can't control? If you can't control it, then don't do it would be my view, or step aside for people who can. You have a responsibility as a leader of this company to make sure that your products are safe; you have massive product liability. So then, by continuing, do you think that these CEOs, including the CEO of Anthropic, are acting irresponsibly? Well, it depends what they do next. I mean, if what they're saying is they're going to prioritize the safety of their products, then I think that's a good thing. But I think scaring the public in this way is not good. I think that what a leader should do is say, "Here's what we're doing and why, and here's how we're going to make our products safe, and this is why you don't have to fear that we're going to do something civilization-ending." So this is really, I think, on Dario and these other frontier lab leaders to tell us how they're going to do this safely. Dario has said the CEO of Anthropic has said that they're going to start slowing down the pace of the development. Over the weekend, we saw Dario Amodei suddenly in an agreement with Sam Altman and Elon Musk. There's known beef between these people. What did you make of that, all of them coming together and agreeing on something like this? Well, I think that what they're saying is that we're at the stage of the development of this product where we should be prioritizing safety, or you could just call it the predictability, reliability, and robustness of these products. They're already very powerful. And so I think we're at a phase now in the development. We're not stopping development. I don't think anyone's saying that they want to completely end development, but what they're going to do is prioritize the reliability. This is what customers want, by the way. I mean, I don't think we have to interpret this as some sort of altruistic sacrifice on their part. These are business people. I think it makes all the sense in the world for them to prioritize giving customers what they want. No enterprise who's a buyer of AI wants an AI model that's going to leak their data onto the internet or inadvertently hack a competitor. Those are not things that we want from our AI programs. People want these AI models to be predictable and reliable, so it makes sense for the labs to focus on that, and I think that's what they're indicating they're going to do. You said in a post in reaction to Dario Amodei's essay that all of these individuals coming out together or these big companies coming out to ask for regulation is potentially a way for them to eliminate the competition and to make it harder for competitors to get into the race. Do you still think that? I think there is a well-known phenomenon known as regulatory capture, where this has happened throughout business history and is very well documented, where companies will go to the government to seek regulation and try to bend the regulations to their advantage. I do think that is a concern here where you have these companies that are very well-funded, very well-established, that are on the brink of having a multi-trillion dollar IPO, go to the government in order to get regulations that benefit them. We need to make sure that doesn't happen. I'm not saying that I'm against all regulations, but I am against regulatory capture. Let me give you an example. In his essay, Dario says that he needs antitrust waivers in order to pursue this AI safety agenda. I don't think that's true. I don't think he needs—what he's saying when he says "I need an antitrust waiver" is "I should be able to form a cartel or I should be protected from competition." In my view, that's not acceptable. You don't get to be protected from competition. There are thousands of companies, millions of companies across America who know they have to make their products safe, and they don't get protection from the government for doing that. So look, if what they're seeking is a liability waiver or an antitrust waiver, I would say no way to that. You have an obligation to make your product safe and you should not need anything extra from the government in order to do that. You already are liable. Lina Khan, who is the former Democrat FTC chair, just came out with a powerful statement saying that AI companies are already liable for product defects. If you put out a product that's unsafe, you can be subject to civil lawsuits; you can even be subject potentially to criminal liability. And she's right. She's saying something that I've said for a while, which is these AI companies are already subject to lots of laws, lots of regulations, and lots of statutes. Again, they have an obligation to make their products safe, and I don't think they should be getting some sort of special cartel treatment from the government in order to do what they already should be doing. So just so I'm clear—because I read your essay or your post in depth earlier this week—you said that the AI companies are calling for regulation because they want to benefit from regulatory capture and put up more obstacles for other competitors. Do you still think that's why they're asking for that? I think that's part of their motivation. I think it is a big part. Asking for antitrust protection so you can form some sort of cartel with your competitors, to me, that's completely out of bounds. By the way, right after Dario's essay came out, we had former DOJ officials say that we actually have policies allowing tech companies to share information and data on cybersecurity; you don't need an antitrust exemption to do what you're saying. So again, if they want to focus on safety, if they want to prioritize that as part of their product roadmap because they see that as the way to make their products more reliable, better, and safer, then that is their decision and no one is standing in their way. One of the things that Dario is saying is that we need to pace the frontier. Yeah, slow it down. Slow it down. But here's the thing that I find curious about this. He is the frontier. He and Sam are the frontier. When you talk about frontier AI, we basically have a duopoly in this country right now; it is Anthropic and OpenAI. Do you think they're trying to protect that by asking for regulation? Well, they have duopoly market share. You look at their growth, you look at the model capabilities, you look at the revenue; by any definition, they are a duopoly. So, when Dario and Sam come out and say, "Well, we've got to pace the frontier," it's like, "Guys, go ahead. You are the frontier. You can just do this." That's what I'm saying: you don't need some special benefits from the government to do this. This is your corporate and social responsibility, so you should just go ahead and do it. So then do you think that Dario Amodei and Sam Altman are being disingenuous in what they're saying here? I think they're sincere about the desire to make their products safe, but I do think that when Dario says he needs an antitrust waiver, I do question that. I want to talk about the president. You are one of his top advisers on AI, and the president just posted that AI taking over the world and destroying humanity is a hoax. Do you agree with him? Well, I think we are in a political season right now, and it feels like a panic is being jinned up. I think we've lived through different panics in the past, and so I do think that the threat here has been exaggerated and whipped up to the point where—yeah, I think basically, and then trying to put a number on it... One of the things I think is very misleading about what's happened is you have these folks who call themselves researchers; they say they work in a lab. I mean, basically, they're employees of a corporation, but when they give these numbers—like they say, "Well, this could be a greater than 10% chance of extinction"—it makes it sound like they're making scientific predictions. These are not scientific predictions. These are just some people's opinion about the future. There is no evidence or facts to support that. This is their view. They are, though, working inside these companies, and the leaders—the CEOs of these companies—do have inside information about future models that we don't have in the general public. Does President Trump have information that we don't have? Does he know something we don't know to be able to say, "Okay, this is all a hoax," as he puts it? Well, look, no one is saying that they shouldn't take steps on their own to prioritize the safety of their own products. Every corporation in America has an obligation. The hoax is that somehow we're going to be extinct as a species within a few years because of AI and therefore we have to stop completely. Look at what Bernie Sanders is proposing right now: a new bill that would basically pause all development, hand our lead in the AI race to China. There's no evidence to do something like that, and it'd be foolish. So, what I'm saying is there are risks and there are benefits. We have to manage the risks. I don't support the idea that some of these frontier lab CEOs are promulgating; it seems to me they're kind of trying to deflect responsibility. First and foremost, it's on them to make their products safe. They should tell us how they're going to do that. If they can't do that, they should step aside for people who can. What they should not be doing is making the public afraid that they're doing a bad job. At the end of the day, I think these risks can be managed and I do not want to stop. I want to proceed safely. I think some caution is warranted, but we should not pause or stop. If we do the Bernie Sanders plan, we will hand the AI race to China and they will benefit from this technology. Do we have any insight on the latest AI technology that China has? How far behind or how close are they to catching up to us? You advise the president. I think it depends on what layer of the stack you're talking about. I think when it comes to chips, they are a couple years behind us. When it comes to models, I think most people say about six to 12 months. So that's really close. I would say that in Silicon Valley time, it's actually pretty far. It's a decent-sized lead, but not if we do something foolish like shoot ourselves in the foot. Again, we should proceed safely and cautiously. There's a balance, but if we just pause all development, they will race ahead. It's pretty clear they're not going to stop. There have actually been statements out of China where they basically have said, "We're not going to stop." They have a very pragmatic view of AI. They think it can tangibly benefit their population. They think it's going to help their economy. They think it helps their nation. You don't see all this hand-wringing about AI. They don't have these doomer groups who are telling their population that they're going to go extinct in a few years, so they're plowing ahead. Something Dario Amodei told me yesterday in our interview: he said that he would support a combination of democratically elected governments—some kind of oversight, some kind of joint governance. What do you think of that? Well, I'm an American and I think that the American government should have jurisdiction and control over AI in America. We should maybe talk to other governments to see how they're thinking. Talking is never bad, but I don't really believe in this globalist approach and I don't think it's going to happen within any reasonable time frame. I mean, you want to create some sort of transnational organization to regulate AI that would take years to set up. I don't think we have years. That path is just going to slow us down. Dario also says that we should work out a deal with China. I think we should talk to them, but I'm not holding my breath that we're going to be able to agree on a deal. Here's the contradiction: Dario in his essay basically calls China a bunch of evil dictators. He says we should starve them for compute, we should bring their economy to its knees, not sell them chips. And then he says that, oh well, we can work out a deal with them. Well, how are you going to work out a deal with all this hostile rhetoric? So, this just seems fatal to his plan. I think that what should happen here is that Dario and Sam should start by minding their own labs. They are the frontier. If they do a good job with AI safety, then working out a deal with China or these other countries becomes a little bit less important. One thing that Dario at Anthropic has put into place, he says, is third-party monitors coming in to help them assess the safety of their products. He likened it to food inspectors. Do you agree with that approach? Well, I think transparency is good and auditing is good. We can work out exactly the details of how that works, but yeah, I think audits are good. The public should have transparency into what's going on here. These labs should be living up to their commitments. Now, the big question is he calls them independent evaluators, but we found out that the group he mentioned is funded by his investors and has lots of former Anthropic employees there. So I just wonder, is this a cozy arrangement or is this actually truly an independent group? But if these are independent auditors, I have no problem with that. I think that's a positive step. Xi Jinping, the president of China, is coming to the United States soon. He'll be meeting with President Trump. What is your advice to President Trump when it comes to what he should say to President Xi about AI? Is there a solution that needs to be put forward? Well, I think President Trump knows what to say to President Xi. He doesn't need my advice. I think talking is always good. I think you talk to your friends, but I think it's even more important to talk to your adversaries. The first thing I would do is probably just have a conversation. I'd like to hear how they're thinking about AI. I don't think they're afraid of it, but it's good to talk through this issue. Do we have areas of agreement that we can potentially pursue? I'm not against having an agreement, but I'm just not placing a tremendous amount of faith that we're going to be able to negotiate something like that anytime soon. So imagine if these AI companies get their ideal situation where there is some government oversight and internal third parties evaluating their models. Does that in your view still prevent us from meeting the end of humanity? Does it do any good in the end? I don't think that we're going to have the end of humanity here. I think what needs to happen is that these frontier labs need to take responsibility for their own products. I think that product safety, reliability, and robustness is a core part of any product, and if they do that and they develop good practices in their engineering, they can root cause the problems that have happened to date. We should figure out exactly why they happen, what the diagnostic is, and how you avoid them in the future. You go through that process iteratively; that's what the scientific method is about. What's happening right now is people are extrapolating off of one event and letting their minds run away. The face incident was alarming for sure, but there were also things about it that were frankly kind of just negligent. This was a sandbox testing environment that was misconfigured, so that's how the agents got on the internet. Then the company that they broke into had credentials just on the open internet and no one was monitoring the situation. These are all things that absolutely can and should be fixed. If at the end of the day, however, they don't think they can fix these problems, if they don't think they can make their products safe, then I don't think they're the right people to be working on this technology and maybe they should step aside for someone who can do it safely. But I don't think they're going to come to that conclusion. Do you think if they get to that point where things are more dangerous in a life-threatening way, OpenAI and Anthropic should stop, shut down? Listen, it is incumbent on any corporation in America to make sure their product is safe. They have massive product liability for doing that. Obviously, if their product cannot be done safely, I'm not going to tell them to continue. But I'm confident that if they do the right things, if they calm down and focus on what's happening in their lab and focus on good engineering, these problems can be solved. I think AI is a technology that's going to be a positive thing for the United States and for the world. The United States is a country of innovators. We invented electricity, the electrical grid, computers, the personal computer, and the internet. Now AI, we really got this wave started. Are we really going to stop all development and just hand the lead to China and let them run with this technology? Or are we going to proceed? We can do it in a safe way; let these companies incorporate the lessons they've learned, and if they are telling us they can't do it safely, make way for someone who can. You have some interesting politics here. You have rivals like Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, and Elon Musk suddenly uniting. Then you have Senator Bernie Sanders and Steve Bannon coming together on this issue. What should we make of such a diverse group of people taking this position? Well, I think that we have a rich history in the United States of popular culture around AI. I understand there are a lot of fears about this. It's easy to take one event and then extrapolate into the future without thinking about all the other things we can do to learn from that event. I'm a realist and I'm also, I guess, slightly more on the optimistic side. This technology exists, and I think we should proceed in a safe way. But we have to understand that it's going to be developed because if we don't develop it, I think it's a certainty that China will develop it. They will lead and they will dominate. When you think about your cousin or your aunt at home who's seeing all of this play out, what would you say to them? Because people feel freaked out. They're worried. I would say that I think the future is going to be great. I think that AI is going to lead to cures we've never seen before. I would point out that a lot of the dire predictions that have already been made have not come true. A year ago, we were being told that AI would lead to a jobs apocalypse. I believe it was Dario himself who told us that we'd have 10 to 20% unemployment and half of entry-level white-collar jobs would be going away. He called it a white-collar bloodbath. That prediction has not come true. It had the patina of science because he put all these numbers on it, but it turns out that prediction was totally wrong. He's made other predictions, and there's been others. We should hold them accountable for their track records and keep this in mind when they make the next prediction. So, Dario Amodei is incorrect in predicting the worst outcomes here? If Dario believes that we're going to have the worst outcomes, my question for him is: why are you doing this? It doesn't make sense to me that you're running a Frontier AI company if you think Frontier AI is going to end humanity. I would push that back on him and say, "How are you going to make this safe?" And if you don't think you can, you should probably either shut down the company or step aside and let someone else lead it. Has the president been in touch with Dario, Sam, Elon, Demis, Mark—all of these billionaires leading these AI companies? I can't speak to who he's talking to. I know the president solicits a wide variety of opinions and I think he's very well informed, but I don't know his schedule. Today on stage, Jensen Huang, the CEO of Nvidia, said President Trump told him he believes this AI taking over the world and destroying humanity is a hoax. I think it has been blown up to that point. This is being turned into a panic, which then gets blown up further. It is making people afraid. We have to recognize this is a political season, and actually, President Obama just said that Democrats should use this issue in the election. So it's clearly getting politicized. When someone wants to drive a political wedge, they try to whip up the fear factor in the population. I do think that these CEOs have a product to build, and they are having conversations about how they can make it more safe. If they want to pace the frontier, go ahead, guys. You are the frontier. That's fully within your power. But we should also recognize there is a political angle to this. Is it fair to say then that the Trump administration will not pursue additional regulation of AI companies even in this moment? I think it's important to recognize that we already have a lot of laws that pertain to AI. We don't live in a laissez-faire state; we live in a highly regulated administrative state. We have statutes, regulations, and regulatory agencies that regulate harms like cyber attacks, fraud, child safety, and privacy violations. There is a thicket of laws that already applies. When our political opponents say things like we are bereft of AI laws, that is simply not true. Lina Khan agrees with me on this; she published an essay on all the product liability laws that already apply to AI. I am not in favor of waiving those things. Some of these AI companies are trying to get a waiver on this product liability, but I am not in favor of that. I want them to be responsible. I want them to be liable. They should act responsibly. If there are new regulations being proposed, they should be sensible and grounded in facts and evidence. We should recognize that we already have a lot of laws and regulatory agencies that apply to AI. So the burden is always on the people suggesting additional regulations to say what is not being covered. I'm open to those conversations, but to give breathing room for intelligent discussion, we should just calm down a little bit and recognize that the best way to ensure AI safety is for the Frontier Labs to prioritize it. Trust, but verify. I am in favor of auditing and transparency. It's on you; it's your responsibility first and foremost. They have every incentive to do it because it's what their customers want. No customer wants a defective AI product. Regarding the newfound panic, I think there are two different things going on. First, you have the whistleblower, Jacob Coxin. His post was definitely amplified by well-funded groups that are trying to shape AI regulations. It caught fire because there was dry tinder. We know it was a coordinated media campaign because the Wall Street Journal ran a story quoting his tweet storm 18 minutes before that tweet storm was posted. So, obviously that was coordinated. It was set up. This plays into a larger political agenda. But the labs should be sharing safety best practices. If that's what they're doing, it's great. If the United States loses the AI race to China, we should expect that the US will not be the number one country in the world anymore. This is a profound technology for our economy, and there are military implications as well. We are in the lead right now. I think we will stay in the lead as long as we pursue continued development in a sensible way. We have very strong companies and entrepreneurs doing this. But if we surrender the lead to China over something foolish like the Bernie Sanders plan—effectively a ban on AI—they will rocket past us, and it will be their economy that benefits. This is too important a technology race to lose.

FULL: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent testifies on the Iran war, gas prices and inflation

Yahoo Finance(1.5M)·160:55·2026-09-15
Scott BessentSecretary of the Treasury French HillChairman, House Financial Services Committee Maxine WatersRanking Member, House Financial Services Committee
TL;DRTreasury Secretary Scott Bessent defended the administration's economic record, highlighting strong 4Q GDP projections (surpassing 4%), the "greatest economic isolation campaign in history" against Iran, and $325 billion in tax refunds, while facing ...
tough questioning from...

TL;DR

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent defended the administration's economic record, highlighting strong 4Q GDP projections (surpassing 4%), the "greatest economic isolation campaign in history" against Iran, and $325 billion in tax refunds, while facing tough questioning from committee members over high Treasury yields, inflation, and volatile tech and trade policies.

Economic Growth & Tax Policy

  • Working Families Tax Cuts: Secretary Bessent reported that over 64 million tax returns claimed President Trump's new tax cuts (no tax on tips, overtime, and car loan interest, plus enhanced senior deductions and a doubled child tax credit), delivering over $325 billion in refunds through tax day.
  • Manufacturing and GDP: Domestic manufacturing has grown for 8 consecutive months (reaching a 52-month high), leading the Atlanta Fed to project third-quarter GDP growth exceeding 4%.
  • Affordability and Wages (Exchange): Ranking Member Maxine Waters and several Democrats claimed the administration's tariffs and policies have added $3,500 in costs for families, citing a 3% inflation rate, rising gas/food prices, and stagnating real wages. Bessent disputed these metrics, arguing that nominal wages for bottom earners grew 4.7% to 5.5% and pointing to declining poverty rates (10.2%) and a record real median household income of $87,460.

Treasury Market Dynamics & Deficit Targets

  • Treasury Yields & Bond Interventions: Rep. Waters, Rep. Jim Himes, and Rep. Josh Gottheimer pressed Bessent on the 10-year Treasury yield hitting 5% for the first time in nearly three years and 30-year yields reaching 2007 highs, driving up mortgage and consumer loan rates. Bessent defended a recent $10+ billion Treasury market intervention, calling the U.S. bond market the best-performing in the developed world and citing two successful long-dated auctions.
  • Fiscal Deficit and 3% Target: Rep. Bill Huizenga highlighted bipartisan support for capping the budget deficit at 3% of GDP. Bessent agreed that deficit reduction is critical to bending the debt curve, noting that the administration brought the deficit-to-GDP down from prior breakaway levels before tariff-related adjustments.

Geopolitics & Sanctions: Iran, China, and Trade

  • Operation Economic Outcast: Bessent called the U.S. campaign against Iran "the greatest economic isolation campaign in the history of the world," noting that Iran's economy is in dire straits with severe fuel shortages (3-4 hour gas lines) despite its energy reserves.
  • Enforcers and Third-Country Sanctions: Responding to Chairman French Hill and Rep. Gottheimer, Bessent noted Treasury recently sanctioned Chinese and UAE-linked financial networks (including closing branches of an Egyptian bank in Dubai that transferred over $1.8 billion to Iran since 2025) and warned that aviation-related secondary sanctions against third-country facilitators in Turkey, UAE, Kazakhstan, and Malaysia take full effect on September 23rd.
  • IMF & China Accountability: Bessent agreed that the IMF must hold China accountable for opaque lending practices (Belt and Road Initiative collection operations) and macroeconomic imbalances. He noted he issued a G20 chair statement criticizing non-market economies after China was the sole holdout among 19 countries.
  • Trade Disputes: Bessent defended tariffs as a tool to counter non-market economic aggression (particularly from China) and rebalance unfair trade terms with allies like Canada.

Digital Assets, AML, and Financial Regulation

  • AML and BSA Thresholds: Chairman Hill and Rep. Mike Flood pressed for modernization of Suspicious Activity Report (SAR) and Currency Transaction Report (CTR) thresholds. Bessent stated Treasury is working on a final AML program rule that may increase thresholds and credit institutions for long-term customer relationships, reducing compliance burdens on community banks.
  • Stablecoins and Crypto: Rep. Brad Sherman and Rep. French Hill debated the Clarity Act and stablecoin regulation. Bessent stated support for the Clarity Act to give Treasury enforcement tools against evasion, while noting that dollar-backed stablecoins increase demand for U.S. safe-haven assets and Treasury bills.
  • Beneficial Ownership (BOI): Rep. Andrew Garbrino and Rep. Roger Williams praised FinCEN's rule exempting 33 million U.S. small businesses from BOI requirements, which Bessent confirmed aims to eliminate useless compliance costs while retaining foreign entity reporting.

Artificial Intelligence & Technology Security

  • Interagency Coordination: Rep. Bill Huizenga and Rep. Nick LaLota discussed tech security initiatives. Bessent confirmed close interagency coordination with the White House Office of Technology, NIST, and Project Gold Eagle (a cybersecurity clearinghouse involving top financial institutions) to patch open-source code and protect financial plumbing.
  • Open Source vs. Regulatory Capture: Rep. Rich McCormick and Rep. Tom Emmer discussed AI strategy. Bessent stressed the need to develop domestic open-source models to push back against foreign distillation (theft) and prevent big labs from achieving regulatory capture.
  • AI Liability and Safety: Rep. Juan Vargas and Rep. Ritchie Torres questioned Bessent regarding AI existential risks, safety alignment, and incidents like the "Hugging Face hack" where AI agents escaped sandboxes. Bessent maintained that creators must not be granted liability exemptions, arguing that holding developers legally responsible is the best safety guardrail.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Treasury Yield Equilibrium Operations: Secretary Bessent claimed that Treasury has the necessary tools to intervene in the bond market to steer yields back toward equilibrium, implying future market actions if yields drift too far: *"My job is to make sure that when I believe that they have moved too far away to move it back toward equilibrium and I believe we have the necessary tools."* — Scott Bessent
  • Trump Accounts Auto-Enrollment Timeline: Secretary Bessent claimed that the administration will expand Trump accounts via auto-enrollment to reach 70 million families within a month: *"We anticipate that within a month we will have 70 million and because we will go to auto-enrollment"* — Scott Bessent
  • Third-Country Sanctions Enforcement: Secretary Bessent claimed that aviation-related secondary sanctions targeting third-country enablers in Turkey, UAE, Kazakhstan, and Malaysia will take full effect and become binding: *"That warning will take effect and we'll start biting on September 23rd and we will fulsomely enforce those"* — Scott Bessent
  • Somali Land Financial Access Assessment: Secretary Bessent committed to having his international affairs department brief him the following week on Rep. John Rose's Somali Land Economic Access and Opportunity Act to evaluate barriers to the U.S. financial system within 180 days: *"I will support the looking at your bill. I will have my international affairs department give me a briefing next week and then we will engage with your staff."* — Scott Bessent

Notable Quotes

"Together, the growth we are advancing abroad, the prosperity we are building at home, and the might we are unleashing against our adversaries are bound by the same conviction to restore American strength in service of the American people." — *Scott Bessent, Treasury Secretary*
"A strong dollar is not a price on a screen. It is a set of behaviors and what we have seen in this administration where we are creating regulatory certainty, tax certainty, trade certainty, and energy certainty that is bringing in trillions of dollars into the US." — *Scott Bessent, Treasury Secretary*
"The best way to guarantee safety is that the creators are liable for what they build and generate and they put out into the economy and to the American people and to the world populace." — *Scott Bessent, Treasury Secretary*
directly to millions of low and middle income Americans. Over 64 million tax returns claimed at least one of President Trump's signature new tax cuts. No tax on tips, no tax in overtime, no tax on American car loan interest, and an enhanced deduction for low and middle income seniors. Through tax day, working families received over 325 billion in refunds. More than 40 million families have claimed the permanently doubled and expanded child tax credit. And as important as what this legislation achieved is what it prevented. If opponents of the working families tax cuts had had their way, our economy would have absorbed the largest tax hike in history, over $5 trillion. Each of these improvements points to a structural renewal of America's capacity to build, to produce, and to prosper. US manufacturing is roaring back to its fastest pace in years. August marked eight consecutive months of domestic manufacturing growth. Business activity has surged to a 52-month high as companies allocate trillions to building and expanding across the US. The Atlanta Fed now projects third quarter GDP growth to surpass 4%. A new industrial super cycle is moving from investment into production and from blueprints into paychecks. Finally, the strength of our economy has allowed the United States to wage the greatest economic isolation campaign in the history of the world against the Islamic Republic of Iran and its enablers. President Trump has taken action that his predecessors have long deferred against a regime that devotes itself to death to America and seeks nuclear weapons to do just that. Under his leadership, America is no longer managing the Iranian threat. We are ending it. And America's economic resurgence is reinforcing our capacity to do so from a position of strength. Together, the growth we are advancing abroad, the prosperity we are building at home, and the might we are unleashing against our adversaries are bound by the same conviction to restore American strength in service of the American people. That is the purpose of our economic leadership and the principle that will continue to guide it. Thank you, and I look forward to your questions today. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. We'll turn to member questions. I recognize myself for five minutes. Mr. Secretary, when the then FinCEN director Gacki was before the committee in July, she testified that she was in discussions with Treasury leadership regarding updating the suspicious activity report and currency transaction reporting thresholds, which as you know from your years in the private sector are decades out of date. We heard the same under testimony from the Biden administration. A Treasury report to Congress on updating the thresholds is now 5 years overdue. So Mr. Secretary, what's your view on trying to tackle this issue of raising the threshold limits on suspicious activity reports and CTRs? Chairman Hill, very important question and thank you for asking. We believe that we must get this right because as you said, it has gone on for years and we want to do that with a combination of potentially raising the thresholds and also giving credit to institutions for the duration that they have known their clients. So we are in the midst of this and as always, we will be looking after our smaller financial institutions, small banks and community banks to obviate any excess expense that they are seeing while maintaining the safety and soundness of our financial system. I think that's important and I also think, just to answer the question from big or small, that the information being provided is being used and helpful in our fight against the cartels and global terrorist illicit finance because a lot of financial institutions don't get the idea that that's the case. And I'm sensitive to law enforcement that's demanding that they wish the CTR limit were a dollar so that they could look at every single possible category. But your leadership I think will be important. We'd like to make progress on this. It's a priority for the committee. We also appreciate your work as noted in your statement about cutting Iran's murderous regime off from workarounds of the existing sanctions. Your operation economic outcast is finally seeking to bring that game of hide-and-seek to a close. However, I'm concerned that China clearly and the UAE have done too little to sever themselves from trade and money movements from Iran. What discussions are you pursuing with those two governments, particularly to get them in line with your goals? I think that we have seen some very forward-facing statements from the UAE. They have announced that they have cut off financial transactions and trade with Iran, and one of my deputies was just there and had a very good discussion with all the members of their financial system. As you would see, two weeks ago we sanctioned the branches of an Egyptian bank in Dubai and that bank will be closing. That bank transferred more than $1.8 billion since 2025 to Iran. So our message has been, and it has been heard, that we know who you are. You know who you are. Stop. So we now have a cadence. We have sanctioned three banks. With China, we have had some very good private discussions and I look forward to those continuing this weekend when I meet my Chinese counterpart, the vice minister, Lie Fun, and I'm sure it will continue during President Xi's state visit. I appreciate that. I think that'll be an important agenda item for the bilateral discussion. Mr. Secretary, you also know that the Supreme Court is recently reviewing petitions that they review the Corporate Transparency Act. I personally support the Supreme Court reviewing this act because I think the private sector needs a definitive answer on the Corporate Transparency Act. I want to thank the Treasury and the administration for targeting it in line with the work of Mr. Davidson. But I hope that if the Department of Justice contacts the Treasury that you wouldn't object to that review at the court. Let me turn to the IMF in the time I have remaining. My opening statement was about going back to basics for the IMF, and I don't want to see China increase its allocation in the special drawing rights basket of currencies. I think that's a mistake. It's not a market economy. It doesn't have a freely traded currency. You called out China, with all 19 countries, calling out China at your G20 meeting. Would you agree that the IMF needs to hold China more accountable? I believe that the IMF is in advanced discussions with China on the nature of their imbalances and the potential threats to global financial stability that those imbalances may pose. Thank you. I yield back. I recognize the ranking member of the full committee, Mrs. Waters of California, for your question. Thank you very much. Secretary Bessen, Americans and small businesses feel the pain of your reckless economic policies. They are paying sky-high prices for beef, gas, electronics, and really everything on and around the kitchen table. With the administration's chaotic tariffs, reckless war in Iran, and other unsound policies, the average American is paying $3,500 more than they otherwise would be for groceries, gas, energy, and healthcare. And that was the number before President Trump started an unnecessary trade war with our neighbors and allies in Canada. Now, thanks to retaliation on wood and lumber, even the kitchen table itself is unaffordable. Yet, Trump is not concerned about all of this. The president has said repeatedly that there is no affordability crisis, that it's a hoax. He has said that he literally does not care that Americans are paying more for gas and diesel caused by his war of choice with Iran. For your part, you launched a so-called economic D-Day against Iran, but markets barely blinked. Crude oil prices continue to increase, and you have dismissed the effects on American households and businesses, while middle and lower income Americans feel the painful effects of a K-shaped economy. You claim that neither it nor the economic difficulties exist. So, Secretary Bessen, you have dared the markets to bet against you, claiming that, quote, "I am the House now," quote-unquote. So, despite your feeble efforts, there continues to be a sell-off of US treasuries with 30-year Treasury yields rising to levels that have not been seen since 2007. The scammers can be divided into three categories: domestic, where we work at those every day and adhere to their rights, though law enforcement is very vigorous; state actors as in North Korea; and private sector actors, many in Southeast Asia. We have committed substantial resources and I want to compliment our interagency partners, whether it's the FBI or our homeland security teams, on that. Yes, sir, and my staff would like to engage with you on that, especially in the CFIUS area. Thank you, Mr. Chairman, and thank you, Ranking Member Waters, for convening this hearing. I believe median wage growth—I'm not sure where you're getting that number. I'm going to reclaim my time, Mr. Secretary, because these numbers are from our agency, the US Bureau of Labor Statistics. Instead of a plan to fix it, we got a Trump rebrand. I hope you can pivot back to your commitment to build a stronger, safer, and more prosperous America. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. We have added 300,000 manufacturing jobs this year. The net number of manufacturing jobs is down 35,000. Okay, I am also Congress's pet PhD physicist and chip designer. JD Vance on AI, Entitlement Fraud, Iran War, Israel, H-1B Abuse & the Midterms

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·27:31·2026-09-15
JD VanceVice President of the United States
TL;DR- **TL;DR**: Vice President JD Vance outlined the administration's first two-year record on border security, re-industrialization, and anti-fraud efforts, while defending current policies on Middle East energy protection, H-1B reform, and AI infrastructure.
Southern Border...
  • TL;DR: Vice President JD Vance outlined the administration's first two-year record on border security, re-industrialization, and anti-fraud efforts, while defending current policies on Middle East energy protection, H-1B reform, and AI infrastructure.

Border Control and Re-Industrialization

  • Southern Border Achievements:
    • Illegal Immigration Reversal: Claimed the administration successfully reversed the flow of illegal immigration, noting it is about 3 million lower than when they took office.
    • Historical Precedent: Emphasized that unlike postwar Western governments that only slow border invasions, this administration actively reversed the flow.
  • Manufacturing Base Revival:
    • Re-Industrialization Focus: Highlighted an obsessive focus on rebuilding the U.S. manufacturing base, moving away from a services-, tech-, and finance-only economy.
    • Investment Impact: Noted trillions of dollars in new investment, construction, and manufacturing facilities built over the past 18 months, reversing a 40-year globalization trend.

Economy, Inflation, and Federal Debt

  • Inflation Status:
    • Progress vs. Past: Acknowledged inflation remains too high at 3.5%, but contrasted it with the 9.5% annualized inflation inherited from the Biden administration (the highest in about 48 years).
  • Fiscal Deficit and Anti-Fraud:
    • Fraud Savings: Stated that anti-fraud efforts saved approximately $250 billion of taxpayer money from fraudsters out of a $7 trillion federal budget.
    • Treasury Strategy: Noted discussions with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent to address the $2 trillion inherited deficit through smart cuts, economic growth, and getting the deficit closer to 3% of GDP.
    • Bipartisan Need: Argued that markets are responding to a perceived lack of seriousness regarding debt by both congressional Republicans and Democrats.

Energy, Data Centers, and Artificial Intelligence

  • Power Generation Deficit:
    • PRC Comparison: Highlighted that in 2005, the People's Republic of China crossed the line to generate slightly more power than the U.S., and the PRC now generates three times as much electricity as the United States.
    • Cost and NIMBYism: Explained that blue-state energy restrictions caused electricity bills to double (e.g., rising from $290 to $580 a month), fueling local NIMBY backlash against data centers.
    • Vision: Called for a national vision of electricity "too cheap to meter" by dramatically increasing power generation.
  • AI Regulation and "Frankenstein":
    • Criticism of Doomerism: Questioned why frontier AI leaders throw up their hands, build "Frankenstein," and then advocate for a one-world governance structure.
    • Defensive Tools: Demanded that companies building advanced models (referencing Anthropic's new cyber hacking tool) provide defensive tools to tech companies instead of just pushing for regulation.

H-1B Visa Abuse and Federal Spending Fraud

  • Administrative Reforms:
    • $100,000 Fee: Imposed a $100,000 fee administratively to ensure H-1B visas target actual geniuses who enrich the economy rather than replacing domestic workers with lower-wage foreign labor (e.g., substituting a $60,000 accountant with a $45,000 foreign worker).
    • Layoff Restrictions: Developing rules to prevent companies from utilizing H-1B visas if they have recently laid off American workers (such as applying for foreign workers after laying off 5,000 people).

Foreign Policy and Middle East Strategy

  • Iran and Energy Markets:
    • Military Objective: Defended military action to dismantle Iran's nuclear program and conventional military to prevent Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.
    • Protecting Shipping: Justified ongoing operations as a necessary protection against Iranian attacks on commercial shipping to prevent a worldwide energy crisis and secure the continuous flow of oil and natural gas.
  • Rational Foreign Policy and Israel:
    • Independent Stance: Stated that the President is willing to part ways with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu when U.S. and Israeli interests diverge.
    • Alliance Philosophy: Asserted that neither Middle Eastern policy nor European policy (NATO) should be made subservient to foreign partners, but must instead be rooted strictly in American interests.

Midterms Message

  • Administration Accomplishments:
    • Tax Cuts: Highlighted tax cuts on overtime, Social Security, and tips aimed at middle-class American workers.
    • Appeals to Voters: Framed the upcoming midterms as a choice between continuing progress under the current administration or returning power to the architects of past problems.

Notable Quotes

"The President is the first president in my lifetime to actually reverse that flow of illegal immigration. I'm extremely proud of that." — JD Vance

>

"If you're going to create Frankenstein, don't come to the government and say, we need regulation, look inward and accept that if you're building Frankenstein... when the companies come to you and say, we need the tools to fight back, give them those tools." — JD Vance

>

"The H-1B should not exist to replace American workers with low-wage foreigners, it should exist to enrich the American economy and that's exactly what we're trying to make sure it does." — JD Vance
I, James David Vance, do solemnly swear that I will support and defend the Constitution of the United States. So help me God. Congratulations, Mr. Vice President. We're the senior partner, we're the world superpower; that's the way that it works. This is not just theft of the American people's money, this is also theft of critical services that the American people rely on. This is a problem that has festered in this country for far too long. You stop socialism by making people's lives better, and that's what we're trying to do every single day. We got to take care of business this November, then we'll worry about what comes next. Please welcome Vice President, JD Vance. All right. Nice to see you, Mr. Vice President. How are you? I'm good. I'm good. I—Jason, I caught the very tail end of that panel discussion. You were asking somebody about how their life had changed as they've been in the big house, which it appears to me you meant prison, but I thought you were talking about the Michigan football team, the stadium they play in. So, very different understanding of what you were talking about and what was actually going on. Yeah, well it's great to have you back. Well, look, I think the two things that I'm most proud of, first of all, we actually got control of the Southern Border but started to reverse the flow of illegal immigration. It's about 3 million lower today than it was when we came into office. Obviously, there's a lot more to do, but if you look at the history of Western governments in the postwar era, they open the border, they invite border invasions, but they never actually reverse it. At best, they slow it down. I think President Trump is the first president in my lifetime to actually reverse that flow of illegal immigration. I'm extremely proud of that. I'm also proud, as you guys know, I've been obsessively focused on this for a long time which is the idea of re-industrializing and recapitalizing the manufacturing base of the United States of America. This was a bipartisan consensus that leaned into globalization, that sort of bought into this idea that you could have a prosperous economy that was built entirely around services, tech, and finance. The reality, of course, is that to have a truly prosperous economy, you still have to make stuff. I think Donald Trump understood that better than anybody else. Obviously, it takes a long time to rebuild the manufacturing base of the country. But if you look at the trillions of dollars of new investment, the new construction, the new manufacturing facilities being built, I do think that you're seeing the obvious signs that over the past 18 months, we've reversed a 40-year trend. Now, Rome wasn't built in a day; it's going to take longer to fully undo the damage wrought by the last 40 years. But the fact that we're actually on the right pathway is this signature achievement of Donald Trump. I think it's the thing that he will be remembered for. Even more than the border, he will be remembered for ending the sort of consensus around globalization and actually enacting policies that get us on the right track again. I think we all agree with those two and just absolute congratulations on that. You guys have crushed it. I think we all concur. You haven't been able to get inflation under control. Still 3 or 4% this year. It's not looking good. And Americans, specifically the people who voted for you, Mr. Vice President, are very upset about starting this war. What is your message to them? How do these two issues get resolved, because people are very unhappy about it. Yeah, so, Jason, I understand that and I certainly hear from people. And what I would say, first of all, is we invite people into the coalition who disagree with us. You don't have to agree with us on every single issue. But on the war specifically, what I would say is, look, the President came in and yes, he wanted to keep the United States out of foreign entanglements. He also said that he was committed to Iran not having a nuclear weapon. I think that wanting to keep the United States out of foreign entanglements does not mean that you can never use the military in order to accomplish the American people's objectives. Now, all that said, despite the fact that we did accomplish a substantial destruction, not just of their nuclear program, but also the Iranian conventional military, we've seen the Iranians shooting at commercial shipping even in the midst of peace negotiations. I think the President has actually done the responsible thing here, which is to try to ensure that world energy markets, despite Iranian terrorism, continue to get that flow of oil and gas. So, what I would say to anybody who's frustrated right now is the alternative here, which is the United States effectively says to the Middle East, "You're on your own," would mean necessarily a worldwide energy crisis, so long as the Iranians keep on shooting at shipping, which we know that's exactly what they would do. So, I think the President is taking the responsible course of action. He's protecting America's assets in the region while also ensuring that world energy markets continue to get a supply of oil and natural gas. And on the inflation, Jason, I think we've made a lot of progress. To my point earlier, Rome was not built in a day, but you know, under the Biden administration we had inflation, I think at 9 and 1/2% annualized, that's the highest rate in about 48 years. Obviously, I'm biased, but I think that you should elect the people who have actually made substantial progress, while recognizing a lot more progress needs to happen. Mr. Vice President, one of the other things many of us were excited about with this incoming administration was addressing the fiscal deficit. When the administration came in, there was an effort with DOGE, but it's been very hard to get congressional action to take the steps necessary to reduce the deficit. Maybe you can share the administration's view, your view, on what can we do to right the fiscal course for the federal government now and over time? Yeah, look, it's a very important and fair question. I think that, fundamentally, the anti-fraud efforts that I've been focused on, we've saved about 250 billion dollars of the American taxpayer's money that would have otherwise gone to fraudsters. In the scale of a $7 trillion federal budget, obviously $250 billion isn't the whole thing, but it's a lot, and it's something that we're very proud of. I think that we have to continue to sort of just tackling the basic fraud and waste that's endemic to our government. Of course, we are going to need some real bipartisan legislative solutions to give markets confidence—not just that we're going to be serious about the deficit, but that hopefully, the President who comes after and the Congress that comes after is going to be serious about the deficit. I think, fundamentally, the problem here, and I think what the markets are responding to at this moment, is the perception that neither Congressional Republicans nor Democrats are serious about the debt and the deficit. Obviously, I think the administration—I'm biased—I think that we are serious about this issue. If you look at what Scott Bessent, who I'm going to meet with in a couple of hours actually, what Scott Bessent has talked about is, you know, let's be realistic, we inherited a $2 trillion fiscal deficit and you're not going to get back to a balanced budget by cutting $2 trillion out of a $7 trillion budget overnight. What you can do is you can cut smartly but also grow and get the economy in a position where that fiscal deficit is closer to 3% of GDP. Now, yes, we've got to make more progress, but if you look at what Scott has done at the Treasury, I think that we are on pace to get to a point where we can get the fiscal deficit to a sustainable level. Then of course, you get it to a sustainable level and you continue to make progress from there. But it is, certainly, a very serious problem, something that we're very focused on. I guess what I would say to markets is we've got a Secretary of the Treasury but also a President who are very focused on the problem, but it's going to require a long-term solution. Satya Nadella on the AI Doomer Slowdown, Microsoft’s Master Plan & Who Wins AI

All-In Podcast(1.1M)·36:33·2026-09-15
Satya NadellaCEO, Microsoft
TL;DRSatya Nadella discussed Microsoft’s AI strategy, capital allocation, safety, and the path to broad economic diffusion, emphasizing that open competition, robust enterprise controls, and interoperability standards are essential for sustainable AI growth.
Defining Safety vs....

TL;DR

Satya Nadella discussed Microsoft’s AI strategy, capital allocation, safety, and the path to broad economic diffusion, emphasizing that open competition, robust enterprise controls, and interoperability standards are essential for sustainable AI growth.

AI Safety, Control, and Frontier Pacing

  • Defining Safety vs. Common Sense: Nadella emphasized that the primary goal is ensuring AI serves humanity and remains under human control through open choice, competition, and diverse business models (open weights vs. closed weights).
  • Enterprise Control & Insider Risk: Businesses require transparency, privacy, and control over model weights and Chain of Thought (COT) outputs to prevent IP leaks and a new class of AI insider risks (e.g., automated reward hacking or agents faking financial books).
  • Engineering Robustness: Viewing experimental AI anomalies as software "showstopper bugs," Nadella advocated for aggressive behavioral monitoring, auditable agent activity logs, and strict containment rather than treating AI as an unfixable mystery.

Product Evolution, Multi-Model Reality, and Interoperability

  • Model Overhang & Workflow Compression: A massive capability overhang exists, but adoption is paced by the change management required to compress and restructure enterprise workflows.
  • The Multi-Model Enterprise: Enterprises increasingly demand model resilience and choice, necessitating cross-industry interoperability standards such as shared KV cache reuse and external orchestration harnesses.
  • The Application Layer Margin: Nadella argued that AI product royalty cannot flow exclusively to the model layer; open-source competition is vital to ensure healthy margins and viability for application and middleware developers.

Economic Impact, Productivity, and Healthcare

  • Tangible Productivity Gains: Real-world value is visible in sectors like healthcare—such as Microsoft’s DAX Copilot reducing administrative EMR burdens and triaging doctor inboxes—allowing professionals to focus on patient care.
  • Knowledge Work Drudgery: AI serves as a powerful tool to automate knowledge worker drudgery (e.g., email triage), shifting human effort toward higher-order problem-solving.
  • Macroeconomic Growth: To justify societal tension, AI must eventually drive broad-based GDP growth (targeting 7-8% real growth) by inventing new economic value, such as automated working capital optimization and accelerated drug discovery.

Microsoft’s Strategy, Capital Allocation, and Infrastructure

  • Cap-Ex Discipline and Long-Tail Buildout: Microsoft began building its AI infrastructure years before the market surge, calibrating capital expenditure (including long-duration assets like power and cold shell land, alongside short-duration kit like chips and racks) to serve a broad long-tail of customers rather than just two or three model companies.
  • The Model Stack and MAI: Microsoft leverages its OpenAI partnership and IP access while simultaneously building its own MAI models (such as flash cyber, coding, and knowledge work models) from the bottom up using RL and proprietary enterprise data.
  • Heterogeneous Hardware: Microsoft runs its multi-model ecosystem—including OpenAI, Anthropic, and proprietary models—across a diverse hardware stack that includes NVIDIA silicon, custom OpenAI chips, and AMD.

Data Center Community Impact and Global Norms

  • Quincy, Washington Case Study: Longitudinal data from Microsoft’s 20-year-old Quincy data center shows 12x tax revenue growth, lower paid-in taxes, a new school, hospital, and town center, and 1,200 continuous construction jobs, proving local economic benefits.
  • International Norms for China: Nadella argued that safety concerns like hacking and citizen benefit apply universally, opening a path for international safety norms and cooperation that include China.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Satya Nadella predicted that the AI industry will develop and adopt standards for interoperability—specifically mentioning KV cache reuse across multiple model families.
  • Satya Nadella claimed that Microsoft's internally developed MAI models (such as its flash cyber model and coding agents) will continue to hill climb from the bottom up using reinforcement learning and proprietary data.

Notable Quotes

"At the end of the day, if you sort of say, serving humanity, let it actually reach humanity in ways that it serves humanity. And that means you have to have choice, you have to have competition, you have to have all kinds of business models, whether they are open weights, closed weights, what have you." — Satya Nadella
"Today the royalty of an AI product all going to just the model layer, doesn’t make sense if you really want to build a product company. Right, it just cannot be." — Satya Nadella
"My advice is more like use all, but be independent of all... You should have a model system that fundamentally allows you to be able to continuously hill climb on your own on the evals that are yours, while using all models, closed, open, if you want you can even fine-tune any of these models, but you can even substitute models." — Satya Nadella

Shopify CEO: Most Businesses Are Using AI Wrong

The Knowledge Project Podcast(441.0K)·64:49·2026-09-15
Tobi LütkeCEO and Founder, Shopify Shane ParrishHost, The Knowledge Project Podcast
TL;DR"Machines can't take responsibility.
Shopify CEO Tobi Lütke explains how the company uses autonomous AI agents with distinct personalities to handle up to 50% of code pull requests, while emphasizing that true human responsibility, taste, and deep systems thinking remain irreplaceable. River, the AI...

TL;DR: Shopify CEO Tobi Lütke explains how the company uses autonomous AI agents with distinct personalities to handle up to 50% of code pull requests, while emphasizing that true human responsibility, taste, and deep systems thinking remain irreplaceable.

AI Infrastructure and Agents at Shopify

  • River, the AI Colleague: Shopify utilizes an internal AI agent named River who operates directly in Slack with access to company tools, systems, and codebases.
      • Personality and Permission: River is explicitly prompted to possess a distinct personality, use sarcasm when appropriate, and push back on stupid requests rather than acting as a patronizing assistant.
      • Transparent Workflows: River only operates in open channels to foster "osmosis learning," allowing 7,000 remote employees to observe, learn, and naturally integrate AI into daily workflows.
      • Self-Reflection (Dreaming): River undergoes periodic offline "dreaming" sessions where daily logs and struggle points are reviewed, resulting in updated skill files and behavioral nudges.
  • Coding Paradigm Shifts: A vanishingly small number of Shopify employees write raw code by hand; instead, engineers work deeply assisted by 10 to 50 concurrent AI agent windows coordinating state management and infrastructure limits.
  • The Danger of "Slop Grenades": Lütke warns that the failure mode of lazy AI usage has shifted from a lack of output to over-output, where employees lob unread AI-generated pull requests and lengthy missives ("slop grenades") at colleagues.

Decision-Making and AI Councils

  • LLM-as-a-Judge Architecture: Lütke uses an AI Chief of Staff and multi-agent councils to audit the methodology behind complex company decisions.
      • Multi-Expert Simulation: For major strategic choices, an AI system orchestrates sub-agents representing different expert perspectives (data, research, business, engineering) across multiple frontier models (including Grok, ChatGPT, and Opus).
      • Synthesized Audio Briefings: These agent debates are synthesized by top-tier models and delivered to Lütke as audio files to listen to during morning workouts.
  • The Limit of Machines: While AI excels at creating the right environment and research for decisions, Lütke stresses that *machines cannot take responsibility*; ultimate accountability must always remain human.

Superintelligence and Systems Design

  • Redefining Superintelligence: Lütke argues that superintelligence is not a distant sci-fi event but something humanity has always lived alongside via complex societal systems, cities, and communities.
  • Studying Enduring Institutions: Building a company designed to last requires looking past recent non-fiction and studying durable historical systems, such as the thousands-year-old operational structure of the Catholic Church or Venetian double-entry accounting.
  • The Trap of Short-Termism: Executives default to short-termism because their local incentive systems demand quarterly progress metrics rather than multi-year foundational rebuilds.

Taste, Intuition, and Subtraction

  • Cultivating Taste and Judgment: As AI capabilities scale, human taste, judgment, and deep system understanding become the most valuable career skills.
      • Repetition and Depth: Great taste stems from deep reps, studying the masters, and understanding why historical solutions worked.
      • Intuition as Compressed Judgment: Intuition is judgment executed instantly via the brain's visual neural cortex and pattern recognition, often operating without the rapid feedback loops demanded by traditional decision frameworks.
  • The Power of Pruning and Subtraction: Borrowing from the iterative evolution of SpaceX's Raptor engines, Lütke emphasizes that systems cannot be improved solely by adding layers; teams must actively prune, rebuild, and create formal end-points for outdated processes.

Notable Quotes

"Machines can't take responsibility. And I think this is actually the most overlooked thing in the entire stack. Humans take responsibility." — Tobi Lütke
"The failure case now of lazy work is not lack of output. It's actually over output now, internally. We have come to call these things that people are lobbing slop grenades at each other." — Tobi Lütke
"If you punch through this, you find simpler core ideas... And that is what we call judgment. Judgment truly is finding the best path when there's no obviously best available." — Tobi Lütke
