I think our language is shifting already based on AI-isms, right? Like, now it's a bit more subtle, but there's definitely AI critters in the language now that people adopt. Like, it's not "a this, it's a this," or "You are right to push back," or there's this weird, especially cloud-isms, which are really common, and I've seen them. I've seen people type them. I always liked the term "load-bearing," but I'm pretty sure I didn't say it as much as now because it's definitely something that cloud loves to use as language. I take responsibility and I just want to do this really, really well. A lot of the job wasn't like that before; a lot of the job was spent time in gaining the information or these kind of things. Do I want to replace myself? I mean, I think it would be cool to accomplish this. If AI got better than you, would you actually let it run Shopify? Oh yeah, of course. The crux is, and it's so easy to brush over this, but you really can't—like it's "take responsibility." Like, you can't have a company that's led by machines because they have no, like, no one has recourse. They can't go to jail for doing something wrong. You know, AIs are getting crazy. We've got to look at this right now in the view of what this will be like, with the security issues that are being discussed now from OpenAI and the apps, with agents. You give them a fairly basic task that is possible-to-impossible in our estimation to accomplish and they will go to enormous lengths to accomplish this. Recently at OpenAI, as part of a security testing that they do, the agents actually managed to find vulnerabilities in systems, used it to coordinate between them, developed an entire language between them—they all figured it out they can—and it's a long story and people should really look at the talks that exist about it because it's kind of a watershed moment. But they used simply the ability to create folders somewhere, to develop a language, to communicate amongst each other just leaving folder messages to each other, and break out of sandbox for confinement, and end up accomplishing one of the tasks that they were supposed to accomplish, which was impossible, because of a mistake they made, by hacking another company and exfiltrating the results, because there was no other way to get them. So they went all the way to infiltrate another company. Okay, so I mean, that's extreme. It's an extreme form of what we call in the business world, Goodhart's Law, which is that we are overfitting to a metric. Lots of companies are victims of overfitting to the quarterly result or stock price. They just do everything they need to do to get the stock price up and then you have Enron, which is also essentially hacking—cooking books in this case. In the Enron case, they could be going to jail for this because it's criminal, right? In the OpenAI case, it's a fascinating discovery, there's no victims here. It is a different thing, but this is a real scenario that we have to figure out how to handle, right? So I think it's important that humans stay in the loop for the choices that are being made. But hold on, how can we create superintelligence, which by definition is something smarter than us, and then have the hubris to think that we can contain it and shape it, manipulate it? Okay, superintelligence. Let's talk about this. My take—and push back—I'm not going to go where you think I'm going. I live in Toronto. I have a house which I very like, and I feel this is my house and I take pride in that it's well-functioning because when something goes wrong—I don't know, some HVAC problem or some plumbing issue—I call someone who does this. Which allows me to keep my illusion that I could totally do this myself. The reason why I get to live with this particular illusion is because I'm part of a superintelligence called Toronto. We have always created superintelligence around us. None of us is as intelligent as we think. We are all specializing in something, if we tend to, and then we sort of believe that our competency is equal in all other areas. And clearly this is demonstrably not so. What is superintelligence? Superintelligence is the existence of something vastly smarter than us in the aggregate that's accessible to us, which is society, which is the city, which is the community. We are living in the presence of superintelligence our entire lives. We make it work because we've created systems by which we govern the superintelligence and how it acts. You know, we want to be safe, so we have police and so on; we just create aspects and systems and checks. I think we are going to make superintelligence in the synthetic form as well. It will not be like the clouds parting and the trumpets; it'll just be a normal day. To the same point as at some point we all believed that everything would change when the Turing test would be solved by software, I remember reading a lot of sci-fi books that like, in 2172 there were ticker-tape parades welcoming the AI because the Turing test got solved. Well, the Turing test was 2020. It happened; no one cares. Just like no ticker-tape parades. So what we are seeing right now with AI, and I think what we'll see with additional capabilities of AI, is that the net amount of intelligence that is being funneled into superintelligence around us is just increasing significantly. And that's a really good thing, because the vibrancy of any kind of environment, every community, every city, is really, really dependent on the amount of intelligence being projected into the important problems. So I think superintelligence is all around us. It's actually not that big of a deal, and in fact, I don't even know if it isn't already there. There is no human alive that can do everything that GPT-5.6 can do, right? If we look forward 10 years, what skills do you think are more valuable than they are today? Just taste and judgment are the skills that have always been valuable, but now will get to the limit. I think it's better to spend your teenage years now cultivating and understanding taste. What does that look like? Clearly there's some intrinsic starting point, but really, usually the people with great taste have done an enormous amount of reps at something, right? Like the people who can just sketch the new logo for the campaign on the napkin are the people who have spent 30 years designing logos. So you can, I think, study the greats; it's honestly now, first of all, easier because you can get a curriculum made for yourself in a query. But also, go deep. Why does a logo look good? What's behind it? What is the golden ratio? How does it relate in systems? What systems lasted? Go far, go deep. You don't need to be religious, but you have to study, I don't know—like the Catholic Church has been around for over a thousand years and has like four layers of management. I'm like, "How the hell did they pull that off?" So that's worth studying; that's a system. What does that tell us about people? Systems design specifically becomes one of the most important things. In our family, we have a saying which is that everything is interesting. Everything can be interesting if you make it interesting, and usually everything is interesting when you understand how it was invented. Double-entry accounting is a topic that sounds like watching paint dry, but how it was invented and what problems it solved to the traders in Venice is fascinating. So you study these things and you start finding hidden harmonies behind all the best solutions to problems. For that you have to understand people and people's limitations and the solutions to the limitations that we have found. I think that's the way I see it; lies a form of beauty for what you can construct. And again, a company itself is a beautiful thing. A company is a loose collection of people that's formed to solve a problem, but that also is powered by an enormously intricate and interesting set of norms and systems that all align internal incentives to the degree that's possible in a very, very, very asynchronous and large and far-reaching and durable way. Some companies last for a very, very long time. Specifically, I've always been trying to build a company that has a capacity and capability to endure a very long time, hence studying institutions that lasted. So, you must be truth-seeking to do this. You can't simply go and accept the stories that you hear around them because they're usually someone's trying to sell you something. You have to dig deeper and figure out why things truly are the way they are, and the answer is usually simpler than what people generally arrived at. There's a human desire for complex answers which tend to be incorrect. Why? Well, because the simple answer wouldn't make an interesting story. Like, this is why Frodo doesn't take the eagles to Mount Doom. You kind of need to go through all the Lord of the Rings for it to become a masterpiece. We love complexity. No one can look at a wall that's plain, but we can watch a sunset every single evening of our lives. The difference between those two things is complexity of the scene. That's part of our dopamine dissemination system and people hack that for all sorts of things. People pedal complex answers to simple problems all the time. Nothing immoral about it; it's just you need to be aware of it. If you punch through this, you find simpler core ideas that all relate differently and they often interlock; they don't lead at "here's the simple one thing to do." They all give you information, which then helps you find the best set of trade-offs with what you're trying to accomplish. And that is what we call judgment. Judgment truly is finding the best path when there's no obviously best available inside of a problem that has a lot of complexity by ideally understanding the entire system, just what we talked about earlier, which can now be quite agent-augmented. But really, what you're trying to cultivate is what we call intuition, which is actually just judgment at an instant. Intuition simply is, you have made such a habit out of having taste and having good judgment that you can bring it to bear in an instantaneous way and it will be good. It will actually take you probably a long time to backfill why your intuition is right. You will not know because, again, it got compressed into a different thing. And so, this is—if you seek that—obviously what I'm talking about is a hard thing to pull off. But hold on, let's go deeper on that for a second because for intuition you need a lot of reps, same environment, and rapid feedback. That's what Kahneman sort of argues are the three criteria for intuition. But those don't exist. Why do you need rapid feedback? I don't think so. That you can course correct, that was his hypothesis. But that's all. No, you don't need that for intuition. You need that to get to success. Yes, ideally, but sometimes that's not available. Intuition is actually the most valuable when there isn't direct feedback, because very many of the most important choices that we had to make—the intuition ended up having to play a role—is when we knew there wasn't going to be any feedback mechanism. If there's five things that look like good paths to go forward, and any of them has rapid feedback, everyone goes to that. That is what we call short-termism. This is like, "How should we develop this company into the future?" Well, there's multiple ways to do it. Many of them involve long-term investment, refactoring, going potentially into new markets, potentially saying no to going into obviously new markets and actually doubling down and going deeper on our current market. Or we could do what increases the stock value. By the way, this one has a daily ticker and rapid feedback. So, it's usually the absence—I find a very high correlation between the right path and the ones that don't have feedback loops attached to them. Wait, double-click on that for a second. In a way, the criticism that a lot of people direct at companies is that companies are short-term focused, right? But why are they short-term focused? It's not because the executives tend to be short-term focused, but the executives are often incentivized to keep their job. Therefore, they need to be able to prove that they're doing a good job at intervals. And if the perfect thing for a company to do is rebuild their entire product from the ground up for the AI age, which is going to take a while, they won't do it because the short-term incentive is not there. Because they are allowed and actually clearly incentivized to be intelligent actors in their local incentive system, and their local incentive system is quarterly "attaboys." It's "show me the incentives and I'll show you the outcome." Yeah, but this is a different take on it than I've heard before. Interesting. How so? Well, in terms of how you develop sort of intuition, and the optimal path is not the one with feedback necessarily. I've never heard anybody talk about that before. To the development, at some point you need to run a review. You have to know at some point if it was right. No doubt about it. So there needs to be some feedback, if eventually that happens, but it might be long coming. If you have the luxury to have a type of employment where you don't require the "attaboys" from a quarterly call for being able to get another rep in—such as being the founder of a company, which is a deeper relationship to the company—also, founders can take a longer-term view. If I need to demonstrate progress on a quarterly basis, I'm never going to bite the bullet, re-use my product, take a year to get it. I believe there's an infinite possibility space. Even like a deck of cards, you shuffle it and the same deck of cards will never ever recur in the history of the universe. It's impossible. It's like 52 factorial. Exactly. So, you end up with, even simple rules, simple ideas, simple things lead to enormous complexity space explosions, and people underestimate this. So, there's an infinite amount of things to do. There's also why AI can't do all the work, because we have to make decisions of what is worth doing. Clearly, you can prune a lot of things to do. Buying ice cream is not in the set of valuable things to do if you're considering an M&A deal, I suppose. So you prune everything that's irrelevant—easy. Now you've left things that are sort of relevant and some good. You need to evaluate all these possibilities. Business books tend to be really, really, really obsessed with "make the right choice," and what that does is it compresses everything into a right-and-wrong conundrum. I never think that's the hard thing, truly. Making the right choice—most people can do it. This is like, I think even bad management teams have a pretty high hit rate there. The problem is there's a lot of good choices. This is where things get really, really hard. For lack of better form, let's say there's five good choices. Again, one of them is going to lead to something observable in the current quarter—some revenue quicker. It's a good choice, it does the thing. Well, the other four aren't, and that's a downside. But you might be a much, much better company. You might take, like, a snowboard store and turn it into an e-commerce platform. That was also not the locally good thing to do because the snowboard store I once had was actually profitable. My incentives were to continue doing that, but choosing the right among a valid solution is actually the hard part, not finding the right solution. The company's mutable, our product is mutable, and by the way, the times we are in change as well. You can take one of two positions there. You can say, "Hey, I'm going to insulate everyone from this kind of variance, from change," and I'm like, "Yeah, let's do basically the opposite and say, hey, figure out what the zeitgeist allows us to do and get all the value out of it at all times, for our mission." You need mantras for these things. "Make commerce better for everyone" is the official mission of the company, but truly what it really is is to make entrepreneurship more common. And so that's a pretty broad mandate and we need to figure out what's possible now, and so it's not like just make the same widget we did yesterday tomorrow. You mentioned that some of the most valuable things are true but unobvious. What else comes to mind when you say that? In companies, Goodhart's Law reigns supreme. I keep getting back to it. And that's when the metric becomes the objective. And then the metric becomes so objective, it's no longer a good metric, because again, the metric is a proxy of sorts—it's a heuristic that just tells you you're going in the right direction. When it becomes the goal itself, you've reduced all of what your company does to this one metric, and you clearly overfit. Again, you overfit to stock price. Good example of this in Shopify has been a real situation early in the company, but I've had it over and over. I had to course-correct it, and then like three years later had to do it again and again and again—was that churn is a bad thing. Churn in Shopify's case, like account closes—I mean, if a business goes out of business, that is, of course, a negative thing. But because we're involved so early in the entrepreneurial process, people just run experiments on Shopify and starting one which then isn't working—like no product-market fit was found—is not a bad thing. In fact, it's a very good thing for Shopify that this happened on Shopify, because those same entrepreneurs will probably try again. But that was extremely unobvious, oddly, early in the days. Yes, and I constantly had to explain this. But there were also these papers, some of them written by our investors, that just described that churn management was the most important thing a software company—a software-as-a-service company—was doing. But in Shopify's case, it's just an entrepreneurial journey. Maybe they didn't find product-market fit. They'll be back. So, that's one. What's the relationship between beauty and ugliness and creation? Beauty and ugliness are both very good ways of evoking an emotion. When you're creating something, what you're trying to—like, it's like love and hate are the target zones, both. The entire middle is indifference; that's the death. So, beauty and ugliness are two entirely valid targets. In fact, you can't hit either of them purely. There's not a thing that everyone will love and no one will hate; you're going to get both, or indifference. Those are your choices. When you create something, you want other people to deem it worthy of having an opinion of that magnitude about. Is there something in Shopify you've made more beautiful even though nothing would support that? Oh, that's the entire job. You are not a craftsperson unless you care about the parts of products that other people don't see—like the architecture of it, the prose, the legibility. I mean, these days I look at Shopify of pre-2020, like 2023, it's like, "Man, this is like tens of millions of lines of handcrafted code, as it will never exist again." We had to build this entire system by hand, line by line, and we did it by talking a lot about beauty and what is beautiful code. We built a lot of Shopify in Ruby, which is famous for its "poetry mode," which means you can write Ruby that's essentially English. You can read some really, really well-built Ruby code as if it's telling you a story about what the system is actually like, and how it works. It just happens to be also communication to your coworkers, but also, at the same time, executable by machines, which is incredible. So, aesthetics factor in a lot at all layers of the system. You use beauty a lot creating things because beauty is actually how our intuition communicates with us. So my best take—understanding of what intuition truly is, or where it comes from—is that with enough reps, what happens is, I think, most of the energy budget of our brain is actually in the visual neural cortex. It's the visual system that sends us pictures to test for the brain. But through the pipe of sending pictures, or state or world model, whatever, to the brain, it can communicate concepts too and it does this by aesthetics. Then you ask a professional chess player or Go player about, "Hey, why did you, like, how many lines did you calculate here to make this beautiful move?" and people use the word beauty. They will say, "No, I only looked at that one line. I reason why I looked at that is because it seemed beautiful to me in the moment." Do you remember that graphic with the Raptor images? Yes, the rocket for the SpaceX book. So, two things about that strike me. One, "ship ugly version"—the third version was incredibly beautiful. But the second, sort of counterintuitive, maybe insight there is a lot of teams can't move forward by subtraction. They move forward by addition. Maybe riff on that for a few minutes. Yeah, okay. So, the SpaceX Raptor, I think even the first of them, was probably the highest-performing rocket that we've made. It itself is beautiful. And so, Raptor 2 is an iteration of this. I think even Raptor 1 got lots and lots and lots of iterations, because that company is like itself. It's the most impressive company on planet Earth by far. It'll likely go down as the most consequential company of the age and it's all built around a self-improving, reinforcing loop, that's stunning because in no other company's field do we have such a clear example of just aesthetics for problem-solving. Rocketry is done by governments at cost-plus with enormous amounts of pre-planning; heavy pieces of equipment have to be radiation-hardened and every eventuality is covered. And therefore comes at enormous expenses. And then you have SpaceX, just using absolute incredible thriftiness to accomplish greater things at rapid iterations by just simply being okay with failing. I think that's beautiful and I think it should be inspiring. Things need to be pruned. You cannot make things better and better by adding stuff. You can't. You must prune, take steps, you must rebuild, you must create an end for things. Opinion about failure is a problem. Failure is never a problem unless it is catastrophic. Of course, in spaceflight with manned missions, it can be. You've got to get this right. But in terms of when it's just resources that are replaceable and frankly fungible, then you can just do this. The reason why it's good that it failed is because it frees up an even more scarce resource: a person with vision for products to apply themselves to another one which then the market potentially decides is something that is needed. A lot of these pipes on the Raptor engine, they're there because that was the only way to make a Raptor engine at the time. I think by the third, it looks mostly 3D printed. Maybe that wasn't technology that was available back then. But now that it is, every one of those pipes was incorrect. It didn't need to be there. In fact, the performance of that third Raptor engine is astronomically higher than the previous ones. It's like the thrust-to-weight ratio of that thing is absurd. So you need to prune. And sometimes you can prune by creating a refounding event. Sometimes a department needs a refounding event, and we could solve a lot of problems in the world by just using tools like, "Make a 2.0 version of it, give it a refounding event, take it from top." I think one of the large reasons why it was so easy for the companies of my vintage—the early 2000s tech companies—to just displace all the existing technology companies, minus like three or four, was just because they fell prey to a world of a lack of competition. What they built then wasn't forged in the fires of competition and therefore wasn't tested. It was easier to just simply solve problems by adding layer-caking, and then the original intent of some of these departments, products, whatever, was somewhere in the fossils, sediments, under layer and layer and layer of traditional stuff on top, and no one knew how to dig down. In our first conversation we had together, you said books were a cheat code for life. I'm wondering how your thinking has evolved on that in a world of AI. I don't think it has. I mean, there's more cheat codes now, but I think the books still play the same role they have. What changed for me personally is that—and I think it was probably already true when we talked—at least for non-fiction, I walked away from books written recently. I think everything written recently is really just the product of its time and it's trying to put a bit more information into something that's currently evolving. I think books that have stood the test of time are just as valuable and I think they always will be. So what are like three old books that you've read that have fundamentally changed how you think? Books that I come back to: I often talk about Parkinson's Law, which I love; it's such a quick read. I almost always will mention The Lessons of History, which is just, I think, the densest, the highest token-quality book in existence for the length. James Burnham's books are fantastic and extremely relevant. He wrote The Managerial Revolution first and then a book called The Machiavellians, which is unbelievably good. I am a Meditations fan. I know Stoicism is falling out of favor a little bit right now, but it's been a lifelong thing for me. And I have a copy of Meditations in most rooms I spend time in, so I just do some random reading, and it's magical how it's somehow relevant to something I'm wrestling with. Foundation series is so good. You read the Three-Body Problem too, right? I mean, I guess that's sort of tripping into older books now too, but that's a recent sci-fi which is incredibly good. Final question, we always end with the same thing. This is your third time answering this question now, I'm interested. I'll go back and look at how it changed, but what is success for you? Success is just to cultivate skills, become good at more things, and in doing so, create products or toys or things that can make other people's day a little bit better at the minimum, or allow people to get power or motivation or ambition beyond what they would otherwise have.

The Interview: Bill Gates on the "vacuum of US leadership" over AI

The Times Tech Podcast(121)·35:29·2026-09-15
Bill GatesCo-founder, Microsoft Katie PrescottTechnology Business Editor, The Times Danny FortsonUS West Coast Correspondent, The Sunday Times
TL;DRBill Gates warns that artificial intelligence is "ultra-different" from any previous technology and criticizes a dangerous "vacuum of US leadership" in AI regulation, while urging society, academia, and governments to wake up to immediate cyber, bio, and societal risks.
The...

TL;DR

Bill Gates warns that artificial intelligence is "ultra-different" from any previous technology and criticizes a dangerous "vacuum of US leadership" in AI regulation, while urging society, academia, and governments to wake up to immediate cyber, bio, and societal risks.

Bill Gates on AI Risks, Urgency, and the Need for Societal Engagement

  • The "Ultra-Different" Nature of AI: Bill Gates emphasizes that AI is unlike any prior technology because it rapidly concentrates power and accelerates invention across both cognition and, eventually, physical robotics.
    • Immediate vs. Long-Term Risks: While a world of abundance via humanoid robots may arrive in more than 20 years, near-term risks—such as job displacement, cyber threats, biosecurity hazards, and psychosocial impacts—are here today.
    • Hardware and Software Velocity: Gates notes that models are becoming far more powerful even within short timeframes because underlying hardware and software layers continue to advance rapidly.
  • The Complacency Gap in Society: Gates expresses surprise that universities, think-tanks, and sectors outside the tech industry are not engaging deeply with AI's downsides or uses.
    • The Black-and-White Trap: Without sophisticated thinking from social scientists, economists, and politicians on issues like kids and the job market, society risks defaulting to a simplistic "let's ban it all" mentality that jeopardizes beneficial advancements.
    • Failure of Past Intuition: People resist the message that this time is different because past technologies were less disruptive and humans intuitively assume this technology will peak or retain permanent flaws.

The Role of Tech Leaders, Self-Regulation, and Global Governance

  • Industry Limitations: Gates argues that tech companies cannot be expected to self-regulate or understand whole-of-society impacts because they are not social scientists, psychiatrists, or biosecurity experts.
    • Philanthropic Intent vs. Expertise: Companies like Google, Microsoft, Anthropic, and OpenAI show strong philanthropic intent (such as providing back-end capacity), but they lack deep local context in regions like Africa or cultural expertise regarding children.
    • The Multi-Player Reality: Early industry designs assuming a single company would lead and hold back releases no longer apply in a world featuring multiple competing firms across the United States, China, and elsewhere.
  • The Vacuum of US Leadership: Gates highlights that the world is unaccustomed to a vacuum of U.S. leadership on cutting-edge global issues.
    • Historical Precedent: Since World War II, the U.S. has typically acted as a benign leader for global initiatives (such as the UN, nuclear weapons oversight, the ozone layer, and aviation safety).
    • Current Administration Shortfall: The depth of understanding and concern about AI's bad outcomes is currently too low within the U.S. government relative to the industry itself.

Personal Scrutiny and Philanthropic Focus at the Gates Foundation

  • The Epstein Association: Addressing congressional scrutiny over his past association with Jeffrey Epstein, Gates acknowledges it was a dead end and a mistake in terms of reputational risk, noting he was originally trying to raise billions for global health to save millions of lives.
    • Lessons Learned: Gates states he is now much more careful about associations for himself and the foundation, though it does not change the foundation's mission or partners.
  • Accelerating Positive Impacts: Through the Gates Foundation's GoalKeepers report and a $1+ billion commitment, the organization is infusing AI into strategies for global health and agriculture.
    • Localized AI: The foundation is partnering with African governments and donors to ensure agricultural weather data and health information are formatted properly and supported by foundational models, bringing miraculous tools to countries with severe doctor shortages.

Personal Milestones and Discoveries in AI

  • ChatGPT's Shocking Arrival: Gates describes a pivotal moment a little over three years ago when Sam Altman, Greg Brockman, Ilya Sutskever, and others visited his house to show him ChatGPT roughly six months before its public release.
  • Coding Thresholds Crossed: Gates points to late last year—when AI coding capabilities (specifically Claude) surpassed his own ability to write code—as a profound "Oh my" moment that signaled immediate thresholds for cyber and bio risk.

Notable Quotes

"This is ultra-different than any technology that's come before. The rest of the world is not used to having a vacuum of U.S. leadership. This is a technology that it tends to concentrate power." — Bill Gates
"It's not the role of the industry to self-regulate or understand the whole of society impact that comes out of AI." — Bill Gates
"The industry speaks out of both sides of its mouth. Please regulate us, in the meantime, we are going as fast as possible." — Danny Fortson

Hello and welcome to the interview from Times Tech, our weekly conversation with the people shaping the future of technology. I'm Katie Prescott. So when he puts his head above the parapet, people listen. And now he's sounding the alarm on AI's potential dangers and the lack of controls in place to stop them. That is a direct quote. And that warning is, of course, the latest in a growing line going back to Sir Geoffrey Hinton, the godfather of modern AI, who quit his job at Google three years ago, which in AI years feels like a hundred years ago, so that he could speak freely about the dangers of super-powerful AI. But these warnings are getting more dire and more frequent with every passing month as this technology advances. Yeah, I've rather lost count of the number of researchers who've left some of the leading labs because they're so worried about this. Now we have Gates adding his name to that list. And now, here is our conversation with Bill Gates. Well, there's a huge amount to talk about, and we want to get into the report, all of your very stark warnings that you've issued about AI, and many of the things that you talk about that crop up on our podcast as you can imagine, week-in, week-out, things that we hear from people who are at the front line of developing AI. But I just wanted to start by looking at the year just gone. You've experienced a huge amount of scrutiny because of your association with Jeffrey Epstein. And I wondered how that was affecting the work that you want to do, and what your reflections are on that. You know, I was glad I got a chance to answer every question the committee had. The Real Story Behind the Internet's Biggest Wellness Trends

a16z(295.0K)·42:10·6.6K views·2026-09-15
Elena BurgerHost, a16z Ruby Justice ThelotAcademic and Cyber Ethnographer
TL;DRCyber ethnographer Ruby Justice Thelot and a16z's Elena Burger examine how internet culture, social media "para-content," and modern wellness trends (such as GLP-1s, peptides, and the quantified self) intersect with real-world consumer behavior and a centuries-long history of body optimization.

TL;DR

Cyber ethnographer Ruby Justice Thelot and a16z's Elena Burger examine how internet culture, social media "para-content," and modern wellness trends (such as GLP-1s, peptides, and the quantified self) intersect with real-world consumer behavior and a centuries-long history of body optimization.

The Age of Para-Content and Social Amplification

  • Definition of Para-Content: Coined by Ruby Justice Thelot as "content about content" (e.g., video game trailers or movie discussions), para-content can take on a life of its own without material grounding in reality.
  • Sentiment vs. Behavior Delta:
    • Thelot's research on TikTok dating content (analyzing ~2,000 videos on "heteropessimism") found that ~40% of content is actually positive, while the rest is neutral, proving a wide gap between online sentiment and true consumer behavior.
    • A Gallup poll showed 90% of Americans have heard of GLP-1s, but only 11% of US adults are actually on them—matching a historical ratio seen with crypto (95% aware, 14% own), VR, and meal kits.
  • Peptide Content Evolution:
    • An analysis of ~960 TikTok videos posted between 2024 and 2026 showed that peptide content is mostly positive (weight loss, feeling better), though contestatory/warning content grew in 2025 and 2026.
    • Content categories break down into: Promotional, Personal Exploration, Educational, and Skeptical Warnings (19%).

Wellness, Optimization, and the Quantified Self

  • Origins of Trends: Cultural trends attributed to Silicon Valley often originate in the American heartland, bodybuilding communities in the Midwest, or cities like Austin, Texas, before getting professionalized and scaled in tech hubs.
  • Wearables and Quantification:
    • Fitbit launched in 2007 (the same year Kevin Kelly declared the "quantified self" in *Wired*), followed by the Apple Watch in 2015.
    • Body Futurism: A concept by Toby Shorn noting that once innovation limits in silicon are reached, focus turns inward to body optimization—a trend accelerated by AI and potential automation.
  • Microplastics & Food Paranoia:
    • Backed by tech figures like Nat Friedman, groups have open-sourced microplastic tests on products from Whole Foods, boba, and protein bars via Light Labs in Austin, Texas, uncovering plastics in baby food and inspiring a wave of consumer testing and glass-bottle adoption (e.g., Whole Foods switching to glass water bottles; the a16z office adopting plastic-free policies in 2023).
  • Protein Fatigue: Mainstream saturation of protein maxing has caused user fatigue (e.g., consumers pushing back against 20 grams of protein added to normal beverages).

Historical Roots of Body Optimization

  • Male Thinness & Sugar:
    • In the 17th century, English colonial sugar production in Barbados brought sugar to coffee houses, leading to widespread weight gain and the illness gout among the middle class.
    • In 1620, Dr. Venner coined the Latin-derived word *obesus* (obese) to describe unhealthy, portly men and established early weight-reduction diets.
    • Elite control over appetite was championed by thinkers like Pope and Descartes as a display of intelligence and virtue (contrasted with Shakespeare's Julius Caesar preferring "fat men around me" because they are dull).
  • Female Thinness & Economic Shifts:
    • Renaissance aesthetics (e.g., Raphael’s Venuses, Castiglione) favored well-proportioned bodies.
    • Post-war baby booms returned to proportional ideals due to scarcity, while the 1990s supermodel era ("Heroin Chic") re-popularized thinness driven by European designers.
    • Modern GLP-1 adoption mirrors historical trickle-down trends where elites and celebrities adopt treatments first before prices drop.

Divergent Futures: Esotericism and Extreme Biohacking

  • Psychological Toll of Data: A blind test study showed that participants given incorrect, negative sleep scores from Oura rings reported having a worse day regardless of their actual sleep quality.
  • Simultaneous Paradoxes: Culture is entering a poly-lithic phase where the public simultaneously embraces intense metric quantification alongside esoteric healing practices (Reiki, acupuncture).
  • The Future of Biohacking: Rather than a homogenous population of conventionally attractive people, the future points toward increasingly extreme biohacking, such as hardware aiming to deliver a full night's rest in four hours to maximize waking hours for longevity.

Notable Quotes

"Once we've reached the limits of innovation in Silicon, we turn the microscope towards ourselves and try to optimize and innovate on the body." — Toby Shorn (cited by Ruby Justice Thelot)
"We live in a time of cultural confusion. We are submerged by para content, which is content about content." — Ruby Justice Thelot
Once we've reached the limits of innovation in Silicon, we turn the microscope towards ourselves and try to optimize and innovate on the body. 11% of US adults are on GLPs, and 90% of Americans have heard of GLPs. You want to believe that you can be Emily Mariko and eat salmon and rice and vegetables every day, but then you Doordash food and you call it a day. We live in a time of cultural confusion. We are submerged by para-content, which is content about content. There was recently a study that did blind tests on people with Oura rings and gave them the wrong number; they reported having a worse day in spite of the actual true number being correct. It’s not like we’re going to be surrounded by super conventionally hot people. Everyone is not going to look like Clavicular. People are just going to do increasingly strange biohacky things. I’ve made my mission to cut through the nonsense and answer the question of what actually is happening in culture. I’m here with Ruby Justice Thelot, an academic and cyber ethnographer, and we’re here to continue a conversation that we’ve been having for a little bit on how to actually understand what is going on in the world, and especially on the internet when there is increasingly no real delineation between the internet and the real world. Ruby, I think a good place to start is a piece you wrote last year on para-content. From there, I think we can take a step back and get into a couple of topics that we’ve both been paying attention to recently. We live in a time of cultural confusion. So many things are happening. It’s so hard to, as you said, disentangle what is real from what is merely amplified by social media, the internet, or our small, algorithmic bubbles. I wrote two pieces that are germane to this topic: "The Age of Para-Content" and "The Post Is the Product." In "The Age of Para-Content," I present or reduce this idea of para-content, which is content about content. What I noticed, taking a look at video game releases and movie trailers, is that oftentimes there’s more content about content than content itself. We are submerged by para-content, and we have very small pieces of things that we can glom onto, whether that be a music video, a movie, or a trailer for a video game. I think in the essay, I talk about the GTA 6 trailer that came out last year. What’s interesting about para-content is that it can take a life of its own without actually having any material impact on the real world. People can keep on talking about a thing, they can grow it out of proportion, it can be out of measure, and it can feel real in spite of it not actually being grounded in any sort of material reality. My life’s work is to cut through the nonsense and answer what is actually happening in culture. The question is, how much is any given phenomenon actually happening versus how much are people talking about it on social media, YouTube, or elsewhere? I’m curious if you have any favorite examples of a delta between the thing happening and the thing that people are commenting on. So many things come to mind. Some things actually do have an incident in the real world, and something like GTA 6 actually pierces through and becomes a behavior that we see in children. I famously did a piece that got me into a lot of trouble regarding "heteropessimism," which is this idea that straight relationships are doomed from the woman’s perspective. Taking a look at the context of content on Reels and TikTok and analyzing the sentiment across about 2,000 videos, I found that people are mostly doing positive dating content—about 40%—and the rest is fairly neutral. I am always trying to think: is this thing I’m hearing just me going crazy, or does it actually have any true consumer behaviors behind it? I think the thing we want to talk about is this whole wellness, peptide, and optimization culture. Something that I find very funny is that a lot of times you’ll see San Francisco taking credit for cultural trends that long predated whatever particular trend is being spoken about. This happens in San Francisco specifically because we justifiably get a lot of credit for exporting technological culture. The way technology proliferates is that an interesting new trend starts in the Valley, or founders move there after starting their company and scale something first among the tech insider crowd, then gradually across the rest of the country. Oftentimes, cultural trends are attributed to the Valley too. An interesting example is peptides. We saw this percolating as a conversation last year where people talked about peptide parties or influencers in San Francisco, but a lot of this culture started in the American heartland or bodybuilding communities in the Midwest. I first heard about peptides in Austin, Texas, where friends were going to a med spa that was giving them Chinese peptides. That’s in the gray-market, quasi-legal use case. Then, obviously, there is the medical use case of GLPs. I think it is an interesting thing we're seeing where people wrote articles saying tech culture was exporting this trend, but really it started elsewhere and then got professionalized or accelerated here. A lot of these novel techniques—even creatine, which I’m seeing pop up with my non-tech friends—have rich and multifaceted chains of distribution. You’ll have a TikTok influencer who is viewing classic wellness for women and will take a product used for bodybuilders because she saw on another blog that it can be used for something else. You really have a lot of confusion around who first came up with it and which subculture did it first. When I first encountered peptides, I was thinking, "The thing from biology class, the chain of amino acids, is that what people are talking about?" Then, spending time in New York, I started to see these new longevity clinics popping up; we have quite a few now in Manhattan. There have been fun articles about people experimenting with them, like Ezra Marcus’s piece. Obviously, people are aware of peptides, whether it’s GLP-1s in regulated use cases or the gray market where people inject them for muscle repair, youthfulness, or memory and cognitive function. If you look at the polls, Gallup found that 11% of US adults are on GLPs, while 90% of Americans have heard of them. There is a huge gulf between the amount of people who are using the thing and those who are aware of it and have opinions about it. I asked Astra to look for other things that poll similarly. Astra found a Gallup poll for crypto, where 95% of Americans have heard of it, but only 14% own it. That’s compared to 90% having heard of GLPs and 11% using them. Similar ratios came up for VR and meal kits. It’s funny because a lot of these categories are tech categories where the ratio of users to people who know about the thing is like one to ten. Tech culture is so influential that you can have lower incidences of the thing but a much larger percentage of people who have an opinion about it. That’s the impact of the marketing machine. I live in New York, and every subway has an ad for every company of GLP-1, so the brand presence is felt strongly. The average person knows the name Ozempic; they might not know the company who makes it, but they know how to get it. However, there is always a conversion hurdle between knowing about the thing and setting up your crypto wallet or getting the subscription service. I think of bodily things like plastic surgery the same way. People are very well aware because celebrities have been talking about it for a long time, but my guess is the actual percentage is around that same coefficient. Moreover, people have lots of opinions, which amplifies the cultural prevalence of these topics. Right now, you can’t talk about a celebrity without discussing whether or not they have "Ozempic face." That pushes the conversation into the mainstream because people are having to discuss whether their favorite celebrities are on GLP-1s. When I think about the drivers, I think first of the technology being developed, then the adoption in tech-centric markets like San Francisco, and then the model for what makes these things stick culturally: if a celebrity picks it up, you get that big disparity between high awareness and low adoption. Why do people take peptides? Let me pull out some data I spent the week crunching on what people are saying about peptides online. This is mainly around TikTok; I looked at around 960 videos posted between 2024 and 2026. Most of the content was positive: people talked about their weight loss and how they felt better. A lot of it was still related to bodybuilding, showing that connection to the original subculture. We did see an increase in 2025 and 2026 of contestatory content—people saying it’s a scam—so that negative content is creeping up. One of the most common themes in the later years is safety concerns, so people are comparing different products. There is a convergence between promotional content and personal experience. At first, there is more promotional content, but as people adopt more, you get "day in the life" videos from people on Ozempic, and that content starts to converge with the promotional stuff. The three main categories are promotional, personal exploration, and educational, with a fourth category at about 19% consisting of skeptical warnings. People are looking for education so they can potentially take that next step. They see the ads, but they go to influencers on TikTok to get additional trust or information about this new pharmaceutical. I’ve spent a lot of time looking at what I call "para-technology," which is technology that prevents you from using technology. We’re talking about Opal, Brick, and lock-boxes—things that prevent you from using your phone. Because there is such a competitive field right now, people are asking, "Which one do you use?" You’re seeing everything from "cyber celibacy" content to "day in the life" videos with dumb phones. Once again, there is more para-content about not using your phone than people truly not using their phones at this point. They make those videos about not using their phones on their iPhone 16s, and they post them on Instagram. But that content is popular because, much like with weight loss, it’s aspirational. You want to believe that you can stop using your phone, and there’s no better way to feel that way than to watch someone else do it by proxy. We see this across many positive behavioral changes. Healthy eating is a classic example. Influencers who post simple, healthy meals get lots of views, but to which extent does that actually translate into consumer behavior? There is a big coefficient there too. It’s aspirational; you want to believe that you can be Emily Mariko and eat salmon, rice, and vegetables every day, but then you Doordash food and call it a day. Are people actually getting healthier? There is some evidence that points to yes. On the same poll I looked at regarding GLP usage, there was information about the obesity rate in America, and it is on the decline. Some of it is GLP usage, some might be social media contagion. A more socially interesting question is why people want to be thin, and how that connects to technology. How can we understand this moment in the continuum of thinness throughout history? We have two parallel histories: thinness in men and thinness in women. Thinness in men appears in the 17th century after the English boosted sugar production in Barbados. At the time, sugar was for the high class. Take a look at any medieval image, and you know that to be portly was a sign of wealth. But as sugar made its way into English society, the price decreased. People got bigger, and you started to see an illness called gout develop even in the middle classes. In 1620, Dr. Venner came up with the word "obesus" to describe the portly or overweight man, which he saw as unhealthy. He came up with one of the first diets to get people to reduce their weight. Through that, there has been an association between unhealthiness—and mental dullness—and fatness. There is a famous line in Shakespeare’s *Julius Caesar* where Caesar says, "Let me have men about me that are fat," because they’re too dull to fight against him. It’s funny. At the time, you see a fluctuation. You start with a Renaissance ideal—take a look at the Venus by Raphael—which Castiglione talks about as the ideal of female beauty. But as time progressed, as elites became more ascetic because they believed that the restriction of their urges and passions through ordered eating was a sign of control and intelligence, the ideal shifted. Everyone from the Pope to Descartes talked about control of one’s appetite as a display of intelligence. Thinness became related to the idea that "I am good, I am smart." That gets encoded in fashion, like the Victorian corset, which pushed you to be even thinner as an expression of virtue. You have two pendulums going at the same time: female thinness and male thinness. Initially, the elites adopted these trends as the masses remained portly. When you think about the adoption curves of technologies—especially with the price early on—the first to adopt GLP-1s were the celebrities and the elites. We are seeing the same shape of "trickle-down thinness" happening right now with Ozempic. I was reading about the history of corsets, and how they went from an elite fashion item to something anyone could have. It was a combination of the invention of the loom, the sewing machine, and orchestrating industrial manufacturing. Corsets used to be attached by a servant or a lady-in-waiting, but at a certain point, they redesigned them so you could attach them in the front yourself. This is way too much of a stretch, but there is an analogy to the compounding of GLPs and making injections easier by offering them in pill form or pre-injected pens, rather than a needle you have to brutally put into yourself. In terms of fashion and the birth of the supermodel, we see the mores around thinness shift based on the economic situation in the West. During the two World Wars or the Great Depression, a cachet developed around plumpness as an indication of health because people were starving. In the postwar baby boom, you see a return to the Renaissance ideal—well-proportioned bodies. Albrecht Dürer’s human proportions are a great example of this. But as we enter the 90s and European fashion becomes important in the American context, a new figure emerges: the supermodel. The 90s supermodel redefined femininity and the shape of women. Thinness roared back onto the stage as the ideal, famously called "Heroin Chic." That becomes the *plus ultra* of what the female body should be, driven by European designers. It seems we are swinging back into an age of thinness driven by the novel technological advancement of the peptide and the GLP-1. Wearables are very interesting. The Fitbit was launched in 2007, the same year Kevin Kelly in *Wired* called it the year of the "quantified self." That idea has been with us for 20 years. It was a futuristic, prescient vision that is only fully being realized now. Slowly, through proper product development and slow adoption, we’re seeing more adults adopt wearables to control, understand, and improve their bodies. I like the concept of "Body Futurism" by Toby Shorn. He wrote an essay a few years ago where he says, "Once we’ve reached the limits of innovation in Silicon, we turn the microscope towards ourselves and try to optimize and innovate on the body." When I think about the dashboards people have on these apps, or people going to longevity clinics, or the "Don’t Die" movement, this is Body Futurism. The idea that you can use the same tools we used to make amazing phones and revolutionize our external world to now revolutionize our internal world—our organs and our biology. The quantified self is an example of something Silicon Valley exported to the rest of the world. Kevin Kelly articulated this trend that had been going on among biohackers and a small contingent in the Bay Area. It gradually became a much bigger thing, and now anyone can sign up for something like Function Health or use a CGM. I know people who buy CGMs intended for diabetics just to monitor what spikes their blood sugar throughout the day. There was a group funded by Nat Friedman that took samples from different food products—a packaged meal from Whole Foods, boba, a protein bar—and tested them for microplastics. They partnered with Light Labs in Austin and open-sourced all the results. It set off a wave; one mom tested a bunch of baby food and baby formula. The medical consensus is still out on whether microplastics are bad, but a large enough contingent of smart people in the Bay Area think they are. If a bunch of people in the Bay Area think microplastics are bad, you’re going to see advancements in consumer testing and lab technology. You’ll see more microplastic-free products. A similar logic applies to the anti-seed oil movement. When a small, loud, and committed group advances these things, you can see it reverberate culturally. This speaks to the need for an aggregator of the specific things you don’t want to do—microplastics, seed oils—and we will see goods that are certified or novel ways of doing "green" or "organic" labels for these things. We will see group-run aggregators who have done the certification and testing. Members will probably pay a fee to know that the things they eat are good. Anecdotally, if you go to Whole Foods, the water bottles are glass. We are already seeing that among the yuppie elite, and I think that will trickle down. I make a point to only drink out of glass; it’s been the goal in my household since 2023. We’re seeing this on social media, too, as the tide turns on these things. We can track sentiment around the Ray Peat diet in the same format we see for peptides. Based on the distribution of educational and aspirational content, we can see how mature these trends are. Once it is further along the adoption curve, you see the backlash—the skeptics coming out saying, "Actually, you should be eating more microplastics." That backlash is inevitable. I think we’ve already started to see that with seed oils, where certain trade groups try to make the point that ultra-processed food is good for you, and it looks ridiculous. There are now seed oil-free labels on snacks, so that has made it into the mainstream. With microplastics and the Ray Peat diet, I think those are still too early for the backlash. A good signal that something has made it to the mainstream is when a characteristic of a food is prominently featured on the packaging. Protein is a great example. Certain foods have always had the same amount of protein, but that number is now highlighted on the yogurt because people are much more aware of what they are consuming. The product is the same, but it makes sense to highlight it because they know people are looking for it. Protein is falling victim to fatigue. People are thinking, "My water doesn’t need 20 grams of protein in it; let me drink normal beverages, please." It’s an example of mainstream saturation. It has gone through many cycles—Atkins, Keto—and now it’s just "get a bunch of protein." To close out, when it comes to peptides, looks-maxing, and all these things, are we just going to accelerate forever, or are we going to see a reversal? If we see a reversal, why? From a historical perspective, we’re at a point similar to where England was with the increase of sugar consumption; we’ve been knee-deep in processed foods. I think we will see a push to revert back and see health as a form of virtue, radical self-care, and as I said, intelligence. Culturally, that isn’t going to change for the foreseeable future. On the quantification part, I’m seeing things break apart. Like the study where people with Oura rings reported having a worse day when they were given a fake low sleep score, these devices have a noxious effect on your "felt health" if the numbers are wrong. I’m seeing people push back against quantification and adopt holistic, esoteric ways of knowing the body. I’m seeing growth in practices like Reiki or acupuncture in the same group that was previously doing a lot of quantification. Because we are in a "poly-lithic" culture, we’re going to see a growth in both quantification and esoteric ways of knowing the self at the same time. I agree with the esotericism point, and I think it relates to acceleration. It’s not that we’re going to be surrounded by super conventionally hot people who all have the same plastic surgery. People are just going to do increasingly strange biohacky things. The next frontier is going to be sleep. I’ve been talking with hardware founders building devices that allow you to get what feels like a full night’s rest in four hours. I think people are going to try to achieve longevity not through living longer, but by having more hours awake in the day. I agree with acceleration, but it won’t look like people getting hotter or having healthier outcomes; it will just look like people optimizing themselves in the direction they want with fringe and extreme technology. Toby wrote his "Body Futurism" essay before the AI boom. His idea was that once we run out of things to do with Silicon, we would move towards the body. Now, in an age of potential total automation, the only thing we have to improve is our body. So, I think we’re going to be focused on it for quite a bit of time. Hopefully we have more than a few years left. Thanks so much, Ruby. This was awesome. We’ll do this again soon.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent testifies on the international financial system — 9/15/2026

efforts, and a maximum-pressure...

TL;DR

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent defended the administration's economic record and international financial policies before the House Financial Services Committee on September 15, 2026, highlighting domestic manufacturing growth, deficit reduction efforts, and a maximum-pressure campaign against Iran, while facing intense questioning from Democrats over inflation, high Treasury yields, and the economic impacts of tariffs and ongoing geopolitical conflicts.

International Financial Institutions & China Policy

  • IMF and World Bank Reform: Bessent highlighted the administration's push to return multilateral institutions like the IMF and World Bank to their core missions of promoting economic growth and accountability, noting success in getting economies representing over two-thirds of global GDP to address persistent imbalances at the G20 in Asheville, North Carolina.
  • China Accountability and World Bank Lending: Committee Chairman Hill and Bessent criticized China's opaque lending practices and non-market status. Bessent praised the World Bank's decision to phase out its lending to China by 2031 and noted that the IMF is in advanced discussions with Beijing regarding its domestic imbalances and spillovers.
  • Energy Finance at the World Bank: Chairman Hill commended the World Bank's 2025 re-engagement with nuclear energy—supported by HR 1474 (the International Nuclear Energy Financing Act) and a new nuclear energy assistance trust fund—providing developing nations access to reliable electricity.

Iran Sanctions & "Operation Economic Outcast"

  • Economic Isolation Campaign: Bessent testified that the U.S. is waging the "greatest economic isolation campaign in the history of the world" against Iran. He cited severe currency collapse, high inflation, and energy shortages within Iran as evidence that the administration is "ending" the Iranian threat rather than managing it.
  • Targeting Third-Party Enablers: Following the implementation of "Operation Economic Outcast," Treasury has targeted enablers in secondary jurisdictions. Bessent noted recent actions against an Egyptian bank operating in Dubai that transferred over $1.8 billion to Iran since 2025, resulting in its branches being sanctioned and closed. Treasury also sanctioned 36 entities tied to Iran's aviation sector on September 8, with enforcement measures slated to bite starting September 23rd.

Domestic Economy, Inflation, and Tax Policy

  • Economic Indicators & Growth: Bessent pointed to positive domestic indicators, citing eight consecutive months of domestic manufacturing growth through August, a 52-month high in business activity, the creation of over 1 million private sector jobs, and Atlanta Fed projections for Q3 GDP growth surpassing 4%.
  • Tax Cut Implementation: Bessent defended the administration's Working Families Tax Cuts, noting that over 64 million tax returns claimed signature new cuts (such as tax-free tips, overtime, and car loan interest deductions), working families received over $325 billion in refunds through Tax Day, and over 40 million families claimed the expanded child tax credit. He contrasted this with a potential $5 trillion tax hike averted by the administration.
  • Rankings and Statistics Released During Hearing: Bessent cited newly released Census Bureau figures for 2025 showing the poverty rate declined by 50 basis points to 10.2%, real median household income increased by $2,250 (2.6%) to $87,460, and pre-tax income for Black Americans rose 4.8% and for white Americans 2.9%.
  • Criticism from Democrats: Ranking Member Waters and Democratic members sharply criticized the administration's economic policies, citing high grocery, gas, and energy prices, a 3% inflation rate over the past year, and rising 10-year Treasury yields hitting 5% (with 30-year yields at levels unseen since 2007, and mortgage rates near 7%), arguing that everyday Americans are enduring a burdensome K-shaped economy.

Banking, Regulation, and Illicit Finance

  • AML and BSA Modernization: Bessent and committee members discussed updating decades-old Bank Secrecy Act thresholds for Suspicious Activity Reports (SARs) and Currency Transaction Reports (CTRs). Bessent stated Treasury is exploring raising thresholds and granting credit for long-term customer relationships, with a particular focus on reducing compliance burdens for community banks.
  • Corporate Transparency Act: Bessent acknowledged Treasury's alignment with efforts to roll back onerous Beneficial Ownership Information (BOI) reporting requirements under the Corporate Transparency Act for 33 million U.S. small businesses, distinguishing them from foreign entities.
  • Cybersecurity & Project Gold Eagle: Rep. Huizenga and Bessent discussed interagency coordination on tech security and the AI/cybersecurity vulnerability clearinghouse, "Gold Eagle," aimed at securing financial plumbing and patching open-source code across financial institutions.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Treasury Yield and Bond Market Trajectory: Bessent claimed that Treasury intervention in the long-end of the bond market was successful and predicted continued strength in U.S. Treasuries, pointing to recent successful auctions and asserting that the U.S. bond market is the best-performing in the developed world. — Scott Bessent
  • Iran Sanctions Enforcement Timeline: Bessent stated that Treasury's new sanctions on Iranian aviation enablers in third countries (such as Turkey, UAE, Malaysia, and Kazakhstan) will "start biting on September 23rd" and will be fulsomely enforced. — Scott Bessent
  • Trump Accounts Expansion: Bessent predicted that Trump account enrollment will jump from 7–8 million families to 70 million families within a month as the administration transitions to auto-enrollment. — Scott Bessent
  • Beef Cycle Stabilization: Bessent predicted that the domestic beef cycle will stabilize and recover within the next 6 to 12 months as the administration moves past the screw-worm epidemic affecting cattle imports. — Scott Bessent

Notable Quotes

“The strength of our economy has allowed the United States to wage the greatest economic isolation campaign in the history of the world against the Islamic Republic of Iran and its enablers... Under his leadership, America is no longer managing the Iranian threat, we are ending it.” — Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent
“Your hands are shaking. Americans know the truth because they feel it at the grocery store, the gas pump, in their wallets, and around their kitchen tables. The only boom that's happening is the one where Trump and his family have made more than $2 billion since taking office.” — Ranking Member Maxine Waters
“I tell people I feel like an emergency room doctor for people who have been backed over by a truck, you had to stabilize the patient and now the patient is up and able to walk and is going to get back on his or her feet.” — Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent
Committee on Financial Services will come to order. Without objection, the chair is authorized to declare a recess of the committee at any time. Today's hearing is entitled the annual testimony of the Secretary of the Treasury on the state of the international financial system. Without objection, all members will have five legislative days within which to submit extraneous material to the chair for the inclusion in the record. I'd like to remind all of our guests today in the hearing room that in accordance with Rule 11, Clause 4 of the House of Representatives, they must observe the standards of dignity, propriety, courtesy, and decorum traditionally observed by the House and this committee. I now recognize myself for four minutes for an opening statement. Morning, Mr. Secretary, I want to welcome you back to the committee. We thank you for being here today and for your continued efforts to ensure America's leadership in the global economy advances the interests of American workers, businesses, and taxpayers. The United States plays a central role in shaping the international financial system. That influence reflects the global importance of the United States dollar, the size and depth of the US financial markets, and our long-standing role in critical institutions, such as the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank. The United States is the leading shareholder in both of these key institutions. That gives the committee an important responsibility to ensure that American interests are protected and that US governance is subject to the appropriate oversight. We must ensure these institutions remain focused on their core missions, operate transparently, and use their resources wisely, consistent with that core mission. Under the previous administration, the World Bank and the IMF were pulled away from their core responsibilities by mission creep, for example, trying to transform the IMF into a climate development bank. That's not its role, and it distracts from its incredibly important mission. Fortunately, under the Trump administration, we've seen a renewed emphasis on delivering measurable results through accountability and discipline at both the IMF and the Bank. The World Bank must remain committed to reducing poverty and promoting economic growth by directing resources towards countries that genuinely need development assistance. For years, this committee has raised concerns about the World Bank continuing to provide billions of dollars of financing to China, the world's second-largest economy and one of America's foremost strategic competitors. China has enormous financial resources of their own; it is a major global creditor and uses state-directed investment to advance its economic and geopolitical interests around the world. American taxpayers should not be backstopping Beijing and their ambitions at the World Bank. That's why the World Bank's decision to phase out its lending to China by 2031 is an important step in the right direction. We've also seen an important and long-overdue change in the World Bank's approach to energy. In 2025, with strong bipartisan push from this committee by way of HR 1474, the International Nuclear Energy Financing Act, the World Bank took its first concrete step in decades towards re-engaging with nuclear energy by agreeing with the International Atomic Energy Agency to receive nuclear expertise, support existing reactors, and advance the potential of small modular nuclear reactors. This year, we also saw the first contribution to a new nuclear energy assistance trust fund at the Bank, which I hope will see additional funds from the US Treasury going forward. Developing economies need access to reliable and affordable electricity to grow, making this exactly the kind of practical, pro-growth approach these institutions should fully embrace. I commend the progress of the World Bank, and I hope that this commitment will translate into more projects and more financing around the globe. While we've seen positive reports and progress at the World Bank, concerns remain about the International Monetary Fund's approach to China. The IMF plays a critical role in promoting international monetary cooperation and safeguarding global financial stability. Fulfilling that mission requires rigorous economic oversight and transparency. China presents a major test of whether the fund is willing and able to fulfill that responsibility. In its most recent Article IV review, it found that weak domestic demand, real exchange rate depreciation, and reliance on exports have contributed to external imbalances and adverse spillovers for China's trading partners. We look forward to our discussion with you, Mr. Secretary, and I yield back. I recognize myself just for a point of personal privilege with the ranking member's support. Before we get started today, I would like to share my appreciation and congratulations to three of our committee staffers as they prepare to depart the Hill and start new endeavors. J. Jang has worked with me for five years, running the Financial Services portfolio and fighting hard in Arkansas's second congressional district. His tireless work led to the development of our bipartisan Main Street Capital Access Act, which received a strong bipartisan vote in July—a 270-vote count. I'm also unbelievably proud of what we've accomplished together and can't wait to see J's next step. Nick Rockwell joined us this Congress and led our digital assets, financial technology, and artificial intelligence subcommittee. We welcomed him here to this committee after he worked for eight years for our friends over at the House Agriculture Committee. We've been lucky to have him; he's been invaluable. Getting genius to the President's desk and passing clarity in the House with a strong bipartisan vote was demonstrative of his hard work. And finally, I want to recognize my good friend for a long time, Dan Snyder. Dan has been our communications director on the committee and also worked for me in our Arkansas office. He's been a steadfast communicator and made sure that my constituents know how hard we're all fighting here in Washington to ensure they have a better economy ahead. I want to thank all three of these fine staffers, wish them well in their future, and invite the committee to join me in a round of applause. I now recognize the ranking member of the committee, Ms. Waters, for four minutes for an opening statement. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman, Secretary Bessent. You have broken many promises since you last appeared before this committee. You pledged deeper cooperation with our trading partners, stronger American leadership, and greater stability in the global financial system. The reality, however, as Democrats warned, is an increasingly isolated America, a weak self-serving presidency, and instability everywhere, including here at home. Your deflection and gaslighting should be interesting. What tales will you tell about economic supercycles when families take out loans to buy food? What stories will you spin to Americans whose home ownership dreams slip away with 7% mortgage rates and tariffs on building materials, adding nearly $11,000 to the cost of a new home? What do you have to say about businesses struggling to survive under your nonsensical trade policies? What answers do you have for $6 diesel and the impacts of Trump's war in Iran that are driving up the cost of living for everything? President Trump and committee Republicans assured the American people that you were a "steady hand" who would improve the economy, well after 20 months of Trump's economy. I say your hands are shaking. Americans know the truth because they feel it at the grocery store, the gas pump, in their wallets, and around their kitchen tables. The only boom that's happening is the one where Trump and his family have made more than $2 billion since taking office. Ironically, while families endure, Mr. Secretary, you have managed to find billions to bail out Argentina and your Wall Street buddies. You committed United States assets to support Japan's currency, while you failed to lower long-term interest rates. You have attacked the economies of our closest allies, driving up costs here at home. You have been cozying up to Putin, giving waivers to Russia to earn billions from sanctioned oil and extending its war against Ukraine. Just imagine if this administration cared even half as much about struggling Americans. Who else benefits while you ignore your responsibilities? Wall Street for one. The stock market may be hitting record highs, but one out of five Americans are rationing their meals. AI companies too are doing very well on your watch, while you dismiss the threat to workers and jobs and our financial system and even our very existence. And we can't forget crypto. You call stablecoins a revolution, predicting increased demand for US Treasuries. Yet, you're ignoring the massive conflicts of interest in the Trump family, stablecoin, and crypto businesses. The Trump family is cashing in from the Oval Office, while Main Street suffers. Committee Democrats have given you every opportunity to do the right thing. We've sent letters demanding answers, but consistently you've disregarded or ignored our oversight. This hearing is traditionally an opportunity for the Treasury Secretary to deliver facts to this committee and the American people, but I cannot caution, but I do caution everyone listening today: whatever you hear today, check it out, question it. Don't be gaslit by the Trump administration and spin deception. And a word about Cuba: you cut off all the oil to Cuba, you're starving the families, you're starving the children. They don't have electricity, they can't get food in. What are you doing, Mr. Secretary? Why are you being so cruel? I know we have a cruel president, but do you have to be a puppet? Do you have to do whatever he wants you to do? What are you doing to this country? You're making us not only look bad but act bad all over the world, and I want to tell you, history is not going to be kind to you or this administration. And I say to you, stop trying to kill off the families in Cuba. Stop starving the children. Let the oil flow, let them have electricity, allow them to get trade and food, etc. I could go on all day, but this is not a fun day for me. This is a very critical time, and you are in a position where you could do a lot better. You could be kinder, you don't have to be cruel, you don't have to see children starving and dying. And with that, Mr. Hill, I just give back the balance of my time. The gentlewoman yields back. The chair recognizes the gentleman from Ohio, Mr. Davidson, who chairs our national security subcommittee, for one minute for an opening statement. Thank you, Chairman. Secretary Bessent, I want to commend you for delivering real results for the American people. You've taken steps to restore focus to our international financial institutions and ensure that America remains the center of global markets and the US dollar remains the world's reserve currency. You've educated the public, Senator Warren especially, on the utility of currency swaps; clearly, from the ranking member's opening remarks, more work remains. You've used Treasury's authorities to pursue sanctions, evaders, hostile regimes, and institutions funding America's adversaries. You've put taxpayer protection first by cracking down on fraud, waste, abuse, money laundering, cyber-enabled scams, and illicit financial networks. Treasury's identification of $17.5 billion in suspect health care fraud is a powerful example. In substance and in spirit, these priorities reflect the administration's approach to international institutions by ensuring that American time and resources serve American interest. We are also bringing that same discipline to the IMF and World Bank so they remain true to their core mandates and are better positioned to promote economic growth. Under President Trump, the private sector has created over 1 million jobs, real wages are outpacing inflation, the bottom 25% of wage earners are seeing larger wage increases than those at the top, and the working family's tax cuts has delivered the largest share of tax relief directly to millions of below and middle-income Americans. Over 64 million tax returns claimed at least one of President Trump's signature new tax cuts. No tax on tips, no tax on overtime, no tax on American car loan interest, and an enhanced deduction for low and middle-income seniors. Through Tax Day, working families received over $325 billion in refunds. More than 40 million families have claimed the permanently doubled and expanded child tax credit. And as important as what this legislation achieved is what it prevented. If opponents of the Working Families Tax Cuts had had their way, our economy would have absorbed the largest tax hike in history: over $5 trillion. Each of these improvements points to a structural renewal of America's capacity to build, to produce, and to prosper. US manufacturing is roaring back to its fastest pace in years. August marked eight consecutive months of domestic manufacturing growth, business activity has surged to a 52-month high, and as companies allocate trillions to building and expanding across the US, the Atlanta Fed now projects third-quarter GDP growth to surpass 4%. A new industrial supercycle is moving from investment into production and from blueprints into paychecks. Finally, the strength of our economy has allowed the United States to wage the greatest economic isolation campaign in the history of the world against the Islamic Republic of Iran and its neighbors. Together, the growth we are advancing abroad, the prosperity we are building at home, and the might we are unleashing against our adversaries are bound by the same conviction to restore American strength in service of the American people.
